The Matrix Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation with a Potential. Thanks: 2010 AMS Subject Classifications: 34L10; 34L25; 34L40; 47A40 ; 81U99. Thanks: Research partially supported by projects PAPIIT-DGAPA UNAM IN103918, IN100321, IA100422, and CONACYT-FORDECYT-PRONACES 429825/2020.
Ivan Naumkin, Ricardo Weder

Abstract
This paper is devoted to the study of the large-time asymptotics of the small solutions to the matrix nonlinear Schrödinger equation with a potential on the half-line and with general selfadjoint boundary condition, and on the line with a potential and a general point interaction, in the whole supercritical regime. We prove that the small solutions are scattering solutions that asymptotically in time, t → ± ∞ , t\to\pm\infty, behave as solutions to the associated linear matrix Schrödinger equation with the potential identically zero. The potential can be either generic or exceptional . Our approach is based on detailed results on the spectral and scattering theory for the associated linear matrix Schrödinger equation with a potential, and in a factorization technique that allows us to control the large-time behaviour of the solutions in appropriate norms.



1
Introduction

In this article we study the large-time asymptotics of the small solutions to
the nonlinear Schrödinger equation on the half-line with general
selfadjoint boundary condition,

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}u=-\partial_{x}^{2}u+Vu+\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,...,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u,\ t\in\mathbb{R},\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{+},

(1.1)

\displaystyle u\left(0,x\right)=u_{0}\left(x\right),\text{ }x\in\mathbb{R}^{+},

(1.2)

\displaystyle-B^{\dagger}u\left(t,0\right)+A^{\dagger}\left(\partial_{x}u\right)\left(t,0\right)=0,

(1.3)

where
\mathbb{R}^{+}:=(0,+\infty),

u(t,x)=\left(u_{1}\left(t,x\right),...,u_{n}\left(t,x\right)\right)
is a function from
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
into
\mathbb{C}^{n},

A,B
are constant
n\times n

matrices, the potential
V(x)
is an
n\times n
selfadjoint matrix-valued
function of
x
, i.e.

V\left(x\right)=V^{\dagger}\left(x\right),\text{ }x\in\mathbb{R}^{+},

(1.4)

where the dagger denotes the matrix adjoint and
\mathcal{N}
is a
n\times n
matrix-valued function. The more general selfadjoint boundary condition at

x=0
can be written as in (
1.3
) where the constant matrices
A;B
satisfy, (
[
6
]
)

B^{\dagger}A=A^{\dagger}B,

(1.5)

and

A^{\dagger}A+B^{\dagger}B>0.

(1.6)

The theory of the matrix Schrödinger equation has its origin at the very
beginning of quantum mechanics. It is important to consider particles with
internal structure such as spin and isospin, and systems of particles such as
collections of atoms molecules and nuclei. A well known example is the Pauli
equation for a half-spin particle. The matrix Schrödinger equation is also
relevant in the theory of quantum graphs, that consist of edges that meet at
vertices. The dynamics at each edge is governed by the Schrödinger
equation. The matrix Schrödinger equation corresponds to a star graph,
that has one vertex and a finite number of semi-infinite edges. Quantum graphs
are important in many problems. For example, in quantum wires, in the design
of elementary gates in quantum computing, and in nanotubes for microscopic
electronic devices. The consideration of general boundary conditions at the
vertices is relevant. For quantum graphs it is crucial that the boundary
condition links the values of the wave-functions and its derivative arriving
from different edges. For our purposes it is also crucial that we study the
general boundary condition because it allows us to consider the case of the
line by means of the unitary equivalence between a
2n\times 2n
system on the
half-line with a potential, and a general boundary condition, and a
n\times n

system on the line with a potential and a point interaction. The case when the
system on the line has no point interaction appears as a particular case of
the boundary condition of the system on the half-line. General references for
quantum graphs are, for example, the monographs
[
10
]
and

[
37
]
. For more information on matrix Scrödinger equations and
related problems, as well as for a detailed discussion of the literature see

[
6
]
.

After the seminal work of I. E. Sigal
[
54
]
,
[
55
]
,

[
56
]
, there is a very extensive mathematical literature in nonlinear
evolution equations, and in particular in nonlinear Schrödinger,
Klein-Gordon and wave equations. For general references see, for example, the
monographs,
[
12
]
,
[
14
]
,
[
28
]
,
[
52
]
,

[
60
]
, and
[
61
]
. General references in the case of
integrable equations, solitons, and the inverse scattering transform are, for
example,
[
1
]
,
[
2
]
,
[
26
]
, and
[
50
]
.
A paradigmatic model of nonlinear evolution equation is the following
nonlinear Schrödinger equation,

i\partial_{t}u=-\Delta u+\lambda\left|u\right|^{\alpha}u,\text{
}t\in\mathbb{R},\text{ }x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\lambda\in\mathbb{R}.

(1.7)

The qualitative description of the solutions to (
1.7
) is far from
being complete. Up to now, various classes of solutions are known. On the one
hand, there exist the so-called low-energy scattering solutions, which
asymptotically in time
t\rightarrow\pm\infty
behave as solutions to the
linear Schrödinger equation
i\partial_{t}u+\Delta u=0
(
[
15
,
16
,
17
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
,
34
,
35
,
36
,
46
,
51
,
58
,
59
]
). On the other hand, there are multisoliton like solutions, which
asymptotically behave like decoupled non trivial trains of solitons. The
soliton like solutions were first explicitly computed in the completely
integrable case
d=1
,
\alpha=2
, and then systematically constructed in more
general settings in
[
42
,
43
,
44
]
. A general approach to
the scattering problem for nonlinear equations is presented in
[
54
]
-
[
56
]
,
[
58
,
59
]
(see also

[
14
,
28
,
61
]
).

Concerning the low-energy scattering of solutions, the cubic nonlinearity

\alpha=2
in dimension
d=1
is a limiting case. The following is known (see,
for example,
[
16
,
17
]
for an overview, and the references there in). In
the case when
\alpha>2
, there is low-energy scattering, that is, a solution
to (
1.7
) with sufficiently small initial value (in some appropriate
sense) behaves similar to a solution to the linear Schrödinger equation
when
t\rightarrow\pm\infty
. On the other hand, if
\alpha\leq 2
, the
low-energy scattering for (
1.7
) cannot be expected
[
9
]
. In
the limiting case
\alpha=2
there is modified low-energy scattering instead,
i.e. small (in an appropriate sense) solutions behave like solutions to the
linear Schrödinger equation modulated by a phase when
t\rightarrow\pm\infty
.

A natural generalization of (
1.7
) for
d=1,
is the nonlinear
Schrödinger equation with a potential

\left\{\begin{array}[c]{c}i\partial_{t}u=-\partial_{x}^{2}u+Vu+\lambda\left|u\right|^{\alpha}u,\text{ }t\in\mathbb{R},\text{ }x\in\mathbb{R},\lambda\in\mathbb{R},\\
u\left(0,x\right)=u_{0}\left(x\right),\text{ }x\in\mathbb{R},\end{array}\right.

(1.8)

where the potential
V\left(x\right)
is a real-valued function. This
equation is related to the Ginzburg-Landau equation of superconductivity

[
24
]
, to one-dimensional self-modulation of a monochromatic wave

[
8
]
,
[
62
]
, propagation of a heat pulse in a solid Langmuir
waves in plasmas
[
57
]
, the self-trapping phenomena of nonlinear
optics
[
38
]
, and stationary two-dimensional self-focusing of a plane
wave
[
11
]
. Equation (
1.8
) is an interesting mathematical
question that requires the development of new methods. Besides, this type of
problems arises in the asymptotic stability for special solutions of nonlinear
dispersive equations, such as solitons, traveling waves, kinks. The nonlinear
equations with external potentials appear in the analysis of the full problem
around these special solutions. Equation (
1.8
) is a prototypical model
of a nonlinear system in a presence of external potentials. We refer to

[
21
]
for a nice survey on asymptotic stability of ground states of
nonlinear Schrödinger equations. The first results in low-energy
scattering for (
1.8
) in dimension
d=1
were obtained in

[
64
]
, and
[
66
]
, where general non-homogenous
nonlinearities were considered, and also the inverse scattering problem of
uniquely reconstruction the nonlinearity and the potential was solved. In

[
64
,
66
]
it is proved that the nonlinear scattering
operator is a homeomorphism from some neighborhood of
0
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
onto itself for a class of weighted
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}})

potentials. In the case of (
1.8
) the results of
[
64
,
66
]
correspond to
4\leq\alpha<\infty.
See also
[
67
]
for
the case of two or more dimensions. For
2<\alpha<4
, in the case of

generic potentials,
the existence of free scattering is proved in

[
20
]
. The existence of modified scattering in the critical case

\alpha=2
for
generic potentials
was studied in
[
23
,
27
,
47
]
. Whereas in
[
19
,
23
,
48
]
, the same problem is
studied for the case of
exceptional potentials
under further symmetry
assumptions on the initial data and the potential. The case of a potential
that gives rise to a partial quadratic confinement is considered in

[
7
]
. Up to now, as far as we know, the case of
general
exceptional potentials
remains open and it is not known whether the symmetry
assumptions needed in
[
19
,
23
,
48
]
are technical or not. The
scattering problem for nonlinear dynamics in presence of trapping potentials,
i.e. potentials that produce negative eigenvalues, is an interesting and
important problem. In
[
65
]
, for the nonlinear Schrödinger
equation on the line, with a potential that produces one eigenvalue, and a
general nonhomogeneous nonlinearity, a center manifold was constructed, and
the asymptotic stability of nonlinear bound states was proven. For the
nonlinear Schrödinger equation on the line, in the case when
\alpha>4,

and when the potential has exactly one negative eigenvalue, the asymptotic
stability of small solitary waves was studied in
[
45
]
. In the
critical case
\alpha=2,
the modified scattering in presence of one bound
state for
generic potentials
was recently studied in
[
18
]
, and
in presence of any number of bound states in
[
22
]
. It is also
worth to mention that the long-time behavior of solutions to a focusing
one-dimensional nonlinear Schrödinger equation with a cubic nonlinearity
and a Dirac potential was studied in
[
25
]
. In the related problem of the nonlinear Klein-Gordon equation with a
potential, the first results in direct and inverse scattering for small
solutions were obtained in
[
63
]
and
[
68
]
. For recent
results in this direction see,
[
40
]
and
[
41
]
.

In this paper we study the large-time asymptotics of small solutions in the
whole supercritical case (
\alpha>2
) for the nonlinear matrix Schrödinger
equation with a potential on the half-line (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) for
both
generic and exceptional potentials
. Besides being important on
its own, as mentioned above, we also have the following motivation to consider
this problem. For the nonlinear scalar Schrödinger equation on the line,
all of the results mentioned above,
[
19
,
23
,
48
]
, for the
case of
exceptional potentials
use in one way or another the symmetry
of the equation, and in particular of the potential, and the symmetry or antisymmetry of the solution, with respect to
x=0
. A useful way to understand
the nonlinear Schrödinger equation on the line without any symmetry
assumptions, is to break the symmetry restricting the problem to the half-line
and to consider the large-time asymptotics of the solutions to the matrix
nonlinear Schrödinger equation on the half-line with
generic and
exceptional potentials
, and with general boundary condition at
x=0.
Then, as
mentioned above, we obtain our results for the matrix Schrödinger equation
on the line using the unitary equivalence between
2n\times 2n
matrix
Scrödinger equations on the half-line with general self-adjoint boundary
condition, and
n\times n
matrix Schrödinger equations on the line with
point interactions. As we already remarked, this is another important reason
to study (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) with general selfadjoint boundary condition. Up to our
knowledge, the only previous results for (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) were
obtained in
[
69
]
, and
[
70
]
in the scalar forced
case, and in particular for the Dirichlet boundary condition. In these papers a
general nonhomogenous nonlinearity was considered. In
[
69
]
the
Cauchy problem was studied, and in
[
70
]
it is proved that the
nonlinear scattering operator is a homeomorphism from some neighborhood of
0

in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
onto itself for a class of weighted
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
potentials, and also the inverse scattering problem of the
uniquely reconstruction the nonlinearity and the potential was solved.
Recently,
[
49
]
obtained dispersive estimates for the linear
matrix Schrödinger equation on the half-line with general selfadjoint
boundary condition, and on the line with point interactions, and with
potentials that are integrable and have a finite first moment. Using these
estimates it is possible to study the scattering operator for small solutions
of (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) for nonlinear interactions of order

\alpha\geq 4,
using the methods of
[
70
]
. However, here we are
interested in nonlinear interactions in the whole supercritical regime

\alpha>2.

1.1
Main results.

Before we state our main results we introduce some notations that we use.

1.1.1
Notation.

By
\mathbb{C}
we denote the complex numbers. For a vector
u=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
in

\mathbb{C}^{n},
we denote by
u_{j},j=1,\dots,n,
its components. For any
matrix
L
we denote by
L^{T}
its transpose. For a linear operator
L
in
a Hilbert space we denote by
D[L]
its domain, by
L^{\dagger}
the adjoint
of
L
and by
|L|
the norm of
L.
We also use this notation in the particular
case where
L
is a
n\times n
matrix. For an open set
U
of real numbers we
denote by
L^{p}(U;\mathbb{C}^{n})
, for
1\leq p\leq\infty,
the standard
Lebesgue spaces of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
valued functions. Further, we designate by

C^{m}(U;\mathbb{C}^{n})
the functions with continuous derivatives up to
order
m,m=0,1\dots.
In the case
m=0
we use the notation
C(U;\mathbb{C}^{n})=C^{0}(U;\mathbb{C}^{n}).
For an integer
m\geq 0
and a real number
1\leq p\leq\infty,

W_{m,p}\left(U;\mathbb{C}^{n}\right)
denotes the standard
Sobolev space. See e.g.
[
3
]
for the definitions and properties of
these spaces. The space
W_{m,p}^{\left(0\right)}\left(U;\mathbb{C}^{n}\right)
is the closure of
C_{0}^{\infty}\left(U;\mathbb{C}^{n}\right)
in the space
W_{m,p}\left(U;\mathbb{C}^{n}\right)
. Here,

C_{0}^{\infty}\left(U;\mathbb{C}^{n}\right)
designates the infinitely
differentiable functions with compact support in
U
. We write
H^{m}\left(U;\mathbb{C}^{n}\right):=W_{m,2}\left(U;\mathbb{C}^{n}\right).
The Japanese
brackets are defined as
\left\langle x\right\rangle=\sqrt{1+\left|x\right|^{2}}.
The weighted Sobolev spaces are defined by

H_{q}^{m}\left(U;\mathbb{C}^{n}\right)=\{u\in L^{2}(U;\mathbb{C}^{n})|\langle x\rangle^{q}u\in H^{m}\left(U;\mathbb{C}^{n}\right)\},q>0.

In the case where
m=0
we use the notation
L_{q}^{2}\left(U;\mathbb{C}^{n}\right):=H^{0,q}\left(U;\mathbb{C}^{n}\right).

If there is no place for misunderstanding, we shall omit
\mathbb{C}^{n}
in writing
the above spaces.
We denote by
\mathcal{L}_{n}
the space of all
n\times n
matrices, and by
L^{1}(U;\mathcal{L}_{n})
the standard Lebesgue space of
\mathcal{L}_{n}
valued functions. We also use the
weighted Lebesgue spaces,

L_{q}^{1}(U;\mathcal{L}^{n}):=\left\{u|(1+x)^{q}u(x)\in L^{1}(U;\mathcal{L}_{n}),q>0\right\}.

When it is clear from the context, we ommity
\mathcal{L}_{n}
in the notation for these spaces. We often
take
U={\mathbb{R}}^{+}:=(0,\infty),
or
U={\mathbb{R}}
.
Further, for a Banach space
\mathcal{B},
a set
G\subset\mathbb{R}^{l},l=1,\dots
, and
m=0,\dots
we designate by

C^{m}(G;\mathcal{B})
the continuous function from
G
into
\mathcal{B}
that have
m
continuous derivatives. In the case
m=0
we use the notation
C(G;\mathcal{B})=C^{0}(G;\mathcal{B}).

We denote the Fourier transform by,

\mathcal{F}f:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,e^{ikx}\,f(x)\,dx,

and the inverse Fourier transform by,

\mathcal{F}^{-1}f:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,e^{-ikx}\,f(k)\,dk.

These formulae make sense for
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}).
For
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
they are defined by a limiting process. By
C
we denote a
positive constant that does not have to take the same value when it appears in
different places. For any set
G
of real numbers we denote by
\chi_{G}(x)

the characteristic function of
G,

\chi_{G}(x)=1,

x\in G,

\chi_{G}(x)=0,

x\notin G.
We define

M=\displaystyle e^{\frac{ix^{2}}{4t}},

(1.9)

and

\mathcal{D}_{t}\phi=\left(it\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\phi\left(xt^{-1}\right).

(1.10)

For later use we introduce the following notation,

\mathcal{T}_{+}(a,T):=[a,T),\quad\mathcal{T}_{-}(a,T):=(-T,-a],\qquad 0\leq a<T\leq\infty.

(1.11)

1.1.2
Statement of our main results.

We now state our main results. First, we define the class of potentials that we
consider in this paper. Let
V(x),x\in[0,\infty)
be a
n\times n

matrix-valued function. For some
N\geq 1,
let us partition
[0,\infty)
into
a finite union of intervals,
[0,\infty)=\cup_{j=0}^{N}\,I_{j},
where

I_{j}:=[x_{j},x_{j+1}),

j=0,\dots,N-1,
with
x_{0}=0<x_{1}<\dots x_{N-1}<x_{N}<\infty,
and
I_{N}:=[x_{N},\infty).
We obtain a fragmentation of the
potential
V
by setting

V\left(x\right)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}V_{j}\left(x\right),

(1.12)

where

V_{j}\left(x\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{c}V\left(x\right),\text{ \ }x\in I_{j},\\
0,\text{ \ elsewhere.}\end{array}\right.

Definition 1.1

The potential
V
admits a regular decomposition
if for some
N\geq 1,
there is a partition (
1.12
) such that the restriction of
V_{j},
to
I_{j}
extends to an
absolutely continuous function in
\overline{I_{j}},

j=0,1,\dots,N-1,

V_{N}
is
absolutely continuous in each interval
[x_{N},b],

x_{N}<b<\infty,
and

<\cdot>^{2+\delta}\,\partial_{x}V\in L^{1}(x_{N},\infty),
for some

\delta\geq 0.

We now introduce the class of nonlinear interactions that we consider in the
present work.

Assumption 1.2

Assume that the function
\mathcal{N}
is defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
that it is
n\times n
matrix-valued, and that
\mathcal{N}\in C^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{L}_{n}\right).
Furthermore,

\sum_{l=1}^{n}\left|\partial_{l}^{j}\mathcal{N}\left(\mu_{1},...,\mu_{n}\right)\right|\leq C\left|\mu\right|^{\alpha-j},\text{ }j=0,1,2\text{,}

(1.13)

for all
\mu=\left(\mu_{1},...,\mu_{n}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and some

\alpha>2.

Let
H_{A,B,V}
be the linear Schrödinger operator associated to problem
(
1.1
)-(
1.3
), i.e., to (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) with
\mathcal{N}
equal to zero (see
Appendix
A
). When there is no possibility of misunderstanding we will
write
H
instead of
H_{A,B,V}
. We denote by
S(k),k\in\mathbb{R},
the
scattering matrix (see (
A.17
)) associated with
H_{A,B,V}.

Let
P_{\pm}
be, respectively, the projectors onto the eigenspaces
corresponding to the eigenvalues
\pm 1
of the scattering matrix at zero
energy,
S(0).
In fact,
\pm 1
are the only eigenvalues that
S(0)
can have.
See Remark
A.3
in Appendix
A
. We define,
m\left(k,x\right):=f\left(k,x\right)e^{-ikx}
, where
f(k,x)
is the Jost
solution (see (
A.15
) in Appendix
A
). Further, we extend the function
m\left(k,x\right)
to
an even function defined for
x\in{\mathbb{R}},
that is,
m(k,-x)=m(k,x),

x\in{\mathbb{R}}.
Observe that
m(k,x)
is defined in
terms of the potential
V,
and that it is independent of the boundary
condition in (
1.3
).

Theorem 1.3

Suppose that the potential
V
is selfadjoint, that
V\in L^{1}_{4}\left(\mathbb{R}\right),
and that
V
admits a regular
decomposition with
\delta=2
(see Definition
1.1
). Further, assume
that the boundary matrices
A,B
, satisfy (
1.5
), (
1.6
), and
that
H_{A,B,V}
does not have negative eigenvalues. Moreover, suppose that
the nonlinearity
\mathcal{N}
fulfills (
1.13
) and that it
conmutes with
P_{-},
i.e.
\mathcal{N}\left(|\mu_{1}|,\dots,|\mu_{n}|\right)P_{-}=P_{-}\mathcal{N}\left(|\mu_{1}|,\dots,|\mu_{n}|\right),\mu=(\mu_{1},\dots\mu_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Then, there is an

\varepsilon>0,
such that for all initial data
u_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap H^{1}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap L^{2}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
that
satisfy the boundary condition
-B^{\dagger}u_{0}\left(0\right)+A^{\dagger}u_{0}^{\prime}\left(0\right)=0,
and with

\left\|u_{0}\right\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\left\|u_{0}\right\|_{H^{1}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\left\|u_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq\varepsilon,

(1.14)

the initial boundary-value problem (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) has a unique solution,

u_{+}\in C\left([0,\infty);H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap L^{2}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\right)\cap C^{1}\left([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\right),

and a unique solution,

u_{-}\in C\left((-\infty,0];H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap L^{2}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\right)\cap C^{1}\left((-\infty,0];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\right).

Further, there exists free final states
w_{\pm\infty}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
with
\|w_{\pm\infty}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\tilde{\varepsilon},
where
\tilde{\varepsilon}:=\varepsilon(1+\varepsilon^{\alpha}),
such that the following asymptotics are valid

\left\|u_{\pm}\left(t\right)-e^{-itH_{0}}\mathbf{F}_{0}^{\dagger}w_{\pm\infty}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\tilde{\varepsilon}\left(\langle t\rangle^{-\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}-1\right)}+\langle t\rangle^{-1/4}\right),

(1.15)

where
\mathbf{F}_{0}
is the generalized Fourier map given in (
A.232
),
(
A.233
), and,

\left\|u_{\pm}\left(t\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}M\mathcal{D}_{t}\left(m\left(\frac{x}{2},tx\right)w_{\pm\infty}\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)\right)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq\frac{C\tilde{\varepsilon}}{\langle t\rangle^{1/2}}\left(\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\langle t\rangle^{-\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}-1\right)}+\langle t\rangle^{-1/4}\right).

(1.16)

Furthermore, the following large-time estimate holds,

\left\|u_{\pm}\left(t\right)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\tilde{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\langle t\rangle^{1/2}}\left(1+\left(\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\langle t\rangle^{-\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}-1\right)}+\langle t\rangle^{-1/4}\right)\right).

(1.17)

Remark 1.4

By Remark 3.8.10 in pages 129-130 of

[
6
]
the potential
V
is generic on the half-line if and
only if there are no bounded solutions to the Schrödinger equation
(
A.1
) with zero energy,
k^{2}=0,
that satisfy the boundary
condition (
A.3
), and it is exceptional on the half-line if and
only if there is at least one bounded solution to the Schrödinger equation
(
A.1
) with zero energy,
k^{2}=0,
that satisfies the boundary
condition (
A.3
). Further, it is purely exceptional on the
half-line if and only if there are
n
linearly independent bounded solutions
to the Schrödinger equation (
A.1
) with zero energy,

k^{2}=0,
that satisfy the boundary condition (
A.3
). The bounded
solutions to the Schrödinger equation (
A.1
) with zero
energy,
k^{2}=0,
that satisfy the boundary condition (
A.3
) are called
half-bound states or zero-energy resonances. Note that the condition

\mathcal{N}\,P_{-}=P_{-}\,\mathcal{N}
is satisfied, in particular, if

P_{-}=I,
or
P_{-}=0.
Further, by Remark 3.8.10
in pages 129-130, Theorem 3.8.13 in page 137, and Theorem 3.8.14 in pages 138-139, of
[
6
]
,

S(0)=-I
if and only if the potential
V
is generic on the half line. In this case

P_{-}=I
. Moreover,
S(0)=I,
and then,
P_{-}=0,
if and only if the potential
V

purely exceptional on the half line. It follows that for generic and purely
exceptional potentials on the half line the condition
\mathcal{N}\,P_{-}=P_{-}\,\mathcal{N}
is always satisfied. Moreover, for a single
equation
\mathcal{N}
is a scalar, and hence, the condition,
\mathcal{N}P_{-}=P_{-}\mathcal{N},
is always satisfied. More generally, in the matrix
case if

\mathcal{N}\left(\mu_{1},...,\mu_{n}\right)=\mathcal{N}_{\text{scalar}}\left(\mu_{1},...,\mu_{n}\right)\,I,\qquad(\mu_{1},\dots\mu_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

where
\mathcal{N}_{\text{scalar}}
is a scalar function, the condition

\mathcal{N}P_{-}=P_{-}\mathcal{N}
is always satisfied.

Remark 1.5

Theorem
1.3
allows us to
construct the inverse wave operators for (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) as
follows. Let
u_{0}
satisfy the assumptions of Theorem
1.3
. Let
us denote

u_{\pm\infty}:=\mathbf{F}_{0}^{\dagger}w_{\pm\infty}.

Then, by (
1.15
)

\lim_{t\rightarrow\pm\infty}\left\|u_{\pm}(t)-e^{-itH_{0}}u_{\pm\infty}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}=0,

and we can define the inverse wave operators
W_{\pm}^{-1},
as follows,

W_{\pm}^{-1}u_{0}:=u_{\pm\infty}.

With the methods of this paper we can also construct the direct wave operators
and the scattering matrix for small solutions. We will consider these problems
in a different publication.

1.1.3
The matrix nonlinear Schrödinger equation on the
line.

As it follows from Section 2.4 of
[
6
]
, a
2n\times 2n
matrix
Schrödinger equation on the half-line is unitarily equivalent to a

n\times n
matrix Schrödinger equation on the line with a point
interaction at
x=0
by the unitary operator
\mathbf{U}
from
L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{+};\mathbb{C}^{2n}\right)
onto
L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{n}\right)

\phi\left(x\right)=\mathbf{U}\psi\left(x\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{c}\psi_{1}\left(x\right),\text{ \ }x\geq 0,\\
\psi_{2}\left(-x\right),\text{ \ }x<0,\end{array}\right.

(1.18)

for a vector-valued function
\psi=\left(\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\right)^{T},

where
\psi_{j}\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{+};\mathbb{C}^{n}\right),

j=1,2.
Let the potential in (
1.1
) be the block-diagonal matrix

V\left(x\right)=\operatorname*{diag}\{V_{1}\left(x\right),V_{2}\left(x\right)\},

(1.19)

where
V_{j},j=1,2
are selfadjoint
n\times n
matrix-valued functions that
satisfy
V_{j}\in L_{1}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).
Under
\mathbf{U}
the
Hamiltonian
H_{A,B,V}
is transformed into the following Hamiltonian on the
line (see
[
6
]
)

H_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbf{U}\,H_{A,B,V}\mathbf{U}^{\dagger},\quad D[H_{\mathbb{R}}]=\{\phi\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{n}\right):\mathbf{U}^{\dagger}\phi\in D[H_{A,B,V}]\}.

(1.20)

Let us write the
2n\times 2n
matrices
A,B
as follows,

A=\left[\begin{array}[c]{l}A_{1}\\
A_{2}\end{array}\right],\quad\left[\begin{array}[c]{l}B_{1}\\
B_{2}\end{array}\right],

(1.21)

with
A_{j},B_{j},j=1,2,
being
n\times 2n
matrices. We have that the
functions in the domain of
H_{\mathbb{R}}
satisfy the following transmission
condition at
x=0,

-B_{1}^{\dagger}\phi(0+)-B_{2}^{\dagger}\phi(0-)+A_{1}^{\dagger}(\partial_{x}\phi)(0+)-A_{2}^{\dagger}(\partial_{x}\phi)(0-)=0.

(1.22)

Let the nonlinearity in (
1.1
) be the block-diagonal matrix

\mathcal{N}\left(\mu_{1},...,\mu_{2n}\right)=\operatorname*{diag}\{\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R},+}\left(\mu_{1},...,\mu_{2n}\right),\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R},-}\left(\mu_{1},...,\mu_{2n}\right)\},\qquad\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},

(1.23)

where
\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R},\pm}
are
n\times n
matrix-valued function.
Then,
u(t,x)
is a solution of the problem (
1.1
)-
(
1.3
) if and only if
v(t,x):=\mathbf{U}u(t,x)
is a solution of
the following
n\times n
system on the line, where we denote

v_{0}:=\mathbf{U}u_{0},

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}v\left(t,x\right)=\left(-\partial_{x}^{2}+Q\left(x\right)\right)v\left(t,x\right)+\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R},\pm}\left(\left|v_{1}(x)\right|,...,\left|v_{n}(x)\right|,\left|v_{1}(-x)\right|,...,\left|v_{n}(-x)\right|\right)v(t,x),

(1.24)

\displaystyle t\in\mathbb{R},\pm x>0,

\displaystyle v\left(0,x\right)=v_{0}\left(x\right),\text{ \ }x\in\mathbb{R},

(1.25)

\displaystyle-B_{1}^{\dagger}v(t,0+)-B_{2}^{\dagger}v(t,0-)+A_{1}^{\dagger}(\partial_{x}v)(t,0+)-A_{2}^{\dagger}(\partial_{x}v)(t,0-)=0,

(1.26)

where,

Q\left(x\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{c}V_{1}\left(x\right),\text{ \ }x\geq 0,\\
V_{2}\left(-x\right),\text{ \ }x<0.\end{array}\right.

(1.27)

For example, let us take
[
6
]
,

A=\left[\begin{array}[c]{lc}0_{n}&I_{n}\\
0_{n}&I_{n}\end{array}\right],\quad B=\left[\begin{array}[c]{lc}-I_{n}&\Lambda\\
\ I_{n}&0_{n}\end{array}\right],

(1.28)

where
\Lambda
is a selfadjoint
n\times n
matrix. These matrices satisfy
(
1.5
,
1.6
). Moreover, the transmission condition in
(
1.26
) is given by,

v(t,0+)=v(t,0-)=v(t,0),\quad(\partial_{x}v)(t,0+)-(\partial_{x}v)(t,0-)=\Lambda v(t,0).

(1.29)

This transmission condition corresponds to a Dirac delta point interaction at

x=0
with coupling matrix
\Lambda
. If
\Lambda=0,

v(t,x)
and

(\partial_{x}v)(t,x)
are continuous at
x=0
and the transmission condition
corresponds to the matrix Schrödinger equation on the line without a
point interaction at
x=0.

We denote,

\mathbf{F}_{0,{\mathbb{R}}}:=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{F}_{0}\mathbf{U}^{\dagger},

(1.30)

w_{\pm\infty,{\mathbb{R}}}:=\mathbf{U}w_{\pm\infty}.

(1.31)

From Theorem
1.3
we deduce the following long-time result for
the matrix nonlinear Schrödinger equation with a potential, and a point
interaction, on the line.

Theorem 1.6

Suppose that the potential
Q
is selfadjoint,
Q^{\dagger}(x)=Q(x),

x\in{\mathbb{R}},
that
Q\in L_{4}^{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
and that
V_{1}(x):=Q(x)
and
V_{2}(x):=Q(-x),

x\geq 0,
admit a regular
decomposition with
\delta=2
(see Definition
1.1
). Further, assume
that the boundary matrices
A,B
, satisfy (
1.5
), (
1.6
), and
that
H_{{\mathbb{R}}}
does not have negative eigenvalues. Moreover, suppose
that the nonlinearity
\mathcal{N},
defined in (
1.23
) fulfills
(
1.13
) and that it conmutes with
P_{-},
i.e.
\mathcal{N}\left(|\mu_{1}|,\dots,|\mu_{2n}|\right)P_{-}=P_{-}\mathcal{N}\left(|\mu_{1}|,\dots,|\mu_{2n}|\right),

\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}.
Then, there
is an
\varepsilon>0,
such that for all initial data
v_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\mathbb{R}^{-})\cap H^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\mathbb{R}^{-})\cap L_{2}^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
that satisfy the transmission
condition

-B_{1}^{\dagger}v_{0}(0+)-B_{2}^{\dagger}v_{0}(0-)+A_{1}^{\dagger}(\frac{d}{dx}v_{0}(0+)-A_{2}^{\dagger}(\frac{d}{dx}v_{0}(0-)=0,

(1.32)

with
A_{1},A_{2},B_{1},
and
B_{2}
as in (
1.21
), and where,

\left\|v_{0}\right\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\mathbb{R}^{-})}+\left\|v_{0}\right\|_{H^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\mathbb{R}^{-})}+\left\|v_{0}\right\|_{L_{2}^{2}({\mathbb{R}})}\leq\varepsilon,

(1.33)

the initial-transmission value problem (
1.24
)-
(
1.26
) has a unique solution,

v_{+}\in C\left([0,\infty);H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\mathbb{R}^{-})\cap L_{2}^{2}({\mathbb{R}})\right)\cap C^{1}\left([0,\infty);L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})\right),

and a unique solution,

v_{-}\in C\left((-\infty,0];H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\mathbb{R}^{-})\cap L_{2}^{2}({\mathbb{R}})\right)\cap C^{1}\left((-\infty,0];L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})\right).

Further, there exists free final states (see (
1.31
))
w_{\pm\infty,{\mathbb{R}}}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\mathbb{R}^{-}),
with
\|w_{\pm,\infty}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\mathbb{R}^{-})}\leq C\tilde{\varepsilon},
where
\tilde{\varepsilon}:=\varepsilon(1+\varepsilon^{\alpha}),
such that the following asymptotics are valid

\left\|v_{\pm}\left(t\right)-e^{-itH_{0,{\mathbb{R}}}}\mathbf{F}_{0,{\mathbb{R}}}^{\dagger}w_{\pm\infty,{\mathbb{R}}}\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})}\leq C\tilde{\varepsilon}\left(\langle t\rangle^{-\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}-1\right)}+\langle t\rangle^{-1/4}\right),

(1.34)

where
\mathbf{F}_{0,{\mathbb{R}}}
is the generalized Fourier map given in
(
1.30
), and,

\left\|v_{\pm}(t)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}M\mathbf{U}\left[\mathcal{D}_{t}\left(m\left(\frac{x}{2},tx\right)\left(\mathbf{U}^{\dagger}w_{\pm\infty,\mathbb{R}}\left(\frac{\dotproduct}{2}\right)\right)(x)\right)\right]\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\frac{C\tilde{\varepsilon}}{\langle t\rangle^{1/2}}\left(\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\langle t\rangle^{-\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}-1\right)}+\langle t\rangle^{-1/4}\right).

(1.35)

Furthermore, the
following large-time estimate holds,

\left\|v_{\pm}\left(t\right)\right\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})}\leq C\tilde{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\langle t\rangle^{1/2}}\left(1+\left(\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\langle t\rangle^{-\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}-1\right)}+\langle t\rangle^{-1/4}\right)\right).

(1.36)

Remark 1.7

The scattering theory for the matrix
Schrödinger equation on the line without point interaction, i.e., when the
matrices
A,B
are given by (
1.28
) with
\Lambda=0,
has been studied
in
[
5
]
. In particular, they consider the low-energy limit of the
scattering matrix for potentials
Q\in L_{1}^{1}({\mathbb{R}}).
In this
situation we can compare the notions of generic and exceptional
potentials on the half-line and generic and exceptional potentials on
the line. We consider the case when the potential,
V,
on the half-line is
block diagonal as in (
1.19
) and where the potential on the line
Q

is given by (
1.27
). Recall that (see Remark
1.4
) the
potential
V
is generic on the half-line if and only if there are no
bounded solutions to the Schrödinger equation (
A.1
) with
zero energy,
k^{2}=0,
that satisfy the boundary condition (
A.3
), that

V
is exceptional on the half-line if and only if there is at least
one bounded solution to the Schrödinger equation (
A.1
) with
zero energy,
k^{2}=0,
that satisfy the boundary condition (
A.3
), and
that
V
is purely exceptional on the half-line if and only if there
are
2n
linearly independent bounded solutions to the Schrödinger
equation (
A.1
) with zero energy,
k^{2}=0,
that satisfy the
boundary condition (
A.3
). Note that for the potential (
1.19
)
with the boundary condition given by the matrices (
1.28
) with

\Lambda=0
the purely exceptional case in the half-line can not
arise, since if we compute the first
n
components of the solution to
(
A.1
) with
k^{2}=0,
using the boundary condition at
x=0
we
obtain the values of the second n components at
x=0
of the solution and of
its derivative at
x=0.
Further, using these initial values we can compute
the second
n
components of the solution. This implies that there can be at
most
n
linearly independent bounded solutions to the Schrödinger
equation (
A.1
) with zero energy,
k^{2}=0,
that satisfy the
boundary condition (
A.3
). Similarly (see
[
5
]
), the
potential
Q
is generic on the line if and only if there are no
bounded solutions to the Schrödiger equation on the line with energy,

k^{2}=0,

-v^{\prime\prime}+Q(x)v=0,\qquad x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(1.37)

Moreover,
Q
is exceptional on the line if and only if there is at
least one bounded solution to (
1.37
), and it is purely
exceptional on the line if and only if there are
n
linearly independent
bounded solutions to (
1.37
). Then, using the transformation given
by (
1.18
) we see that
Q
is generic on the line if
and only if
V
is generic on the half-line, that
Q
is
exceptional on the line if and only if
V
is exceptional on
the half-line, and that
Q
is purely exceptional on the line if and
only if there are
n
linearly independent bounded solutions to the
Schrödinger equation (
A.1
) with zero energy,
k^{2}=0,
that
satisfy the boundary condition (
A.3
).

Remark 1.8

follows from Remarks
1.4
and
1.7
that in the

generic case
the condition
P_{-}\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}P_{-}
in Theorem

1.6
is always satisfied.

Corollary 1.9

Suppose that in (
1.23
) we take
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathbb{R}},+}=\mathcal{N}_{{\mathbb{R}},-}=\mathcal{N}_{{\mathbb{R}}}.

Then, for all even and odd solutions the initial-transmission problem
(
1.24
)-(
1.26
) is equivalent to the following
initial-transmission problem

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}v\left(t,x\right)=\left(-\partial_{x}^{2}+Q\left(x\right)\right)v\left(t,x\right)+\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}(\left|v_{1}(x)\right|,\dots,\left|v_{n}(x)\right|)v(t,x),t,x\in\mathbb{R},

(1.38)

\displaystyle v\left(0,x\right)=v_{0}\left(x\right),\text{ \ }x\in\mathbb{R},

(1.39)

\displaystyle-B_{1}^{\dagger}v(t,0+)-B_{2}^{\dagger}v(t,0-)+A_{1}^{\dagger}(\partial_{x}v)(t,0+)-A_{2}^{\dagger}(\partial_{x}v)(t,0-)=0,

(1.40)

where

\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}(|\mu_{1}|,...,|\mu_{n}|):=\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R}}\left(|\mu_{1}|,...,|\mu_{n}|,|\mu_{1}|,...,|\mu_{n}|\right),\qquad(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Then, the results of Theorem
1.6
apply to all even and odd
solutions to the initial-transmission problem (
1.38
)-
(
1.40
). Moreover, if
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathbb{R}}}=\mathcal{N}_{\text{scalar}}\,I,
with
\mathcal{N}_{\text{scalar}}
a scalar function
from
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
into
\mathbb{C}
and
I
the
n\times n
identity
matrix, the nonlinearity
\mathcal{N}
given in (
1.23
) is equal to

\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{\text{scalar}}\,I,
where now
I
is the

2n\times 2n
identity matrix. Hence, in this case the condition
P_{-}\,\mathcal{N}=P_{-}\,\mathcal{N},
is always satisfied. This corollary shows
how the condition that the solutions are even or odd appears naturally for the
local nonlinear Schrödinger equations on the line.

Corollary 1.10

Let us take a
2n\times 2n
system on the half-line with the
nonlinearity as (
1.23
), with
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathbb{R}},+}=\mathcal{N}_{{\mathbb{R}},+}(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n}),
i.e. independent of

\mu_{n+1},\dots,\mu_{2n},
and
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathbb{R}},-}=\mathcal{N}_{{\mathbb{R}},-}(\mu_{2n+1},\dots,\mu_{2n}),
i.e. independent of
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n}.
In this case the initial-transmission problem
(
1.24
)-(
1.26
) is equivalent to the following
initial-transmission problem,

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}v\left(t,x\right)=\left(-\partial_{x}^{2}+Q\left(x\right)\right)v\left(t,x\right)+\mathcal{N}_{{\mathbb{R}},\pm}\left(\left|v_{1}(x)\right|,\dots,\left|v_{n}(x)\right|\right)v,\ t\in\mathbb{R},\,\pm x>0,

(1.41)

\displaystyle v\left(0,x\right)=v_{0}\left(x\right),\text{ \ }x\in\mathbb{R},

(1.42)

\displaystyle-B_{1}^{\dagger}v(t,0+)-B_{2}^{\dagger}v(t,0-)+A_{1}^{\dagger}(\partial_{x}v)(t,0+)-A_{2}^{\dagger}(\partial_{x}v)(t,0-)=0.

(1.43)

If furthermore,
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathbb{R}},+}=\mathcal{N}_{{\mathbb{R}},-}=\mathcal{N}_{{\mathbb{R}}},
the initial-transmission problem
(
1.24
)-(
1.26
) is equivalent to the following
initial-transmission problem,

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}v\left(t,x\right)=\left(-\partial_{x}^{2}+Q\left(x\right)\right)v\left(t,x\right)+\mathcal{N}_{{\mathbb{R}}}\left(\left|v_{1}(x)\right|,\dots,\left|v_{n}(x)\right|\right)v,\ t\in\mathbb{R},\,x\in{\mathbb{R}},

(1.44)

\displaystyle v\left(0,x\right)=v_{0}\left(x\right),\text{ \ }x\in\mathbb{R},

(1.45)

\displaystyle-B_{1}^{\dagger}v(t,0+)-B_{2}^{\dagger}v(t,0-)+A_{1}^{\dagger}(\partial_{x}v)(t,0+)-A_{2}^{\dagger}(\partial_{x}v)(t,0-)=0.

(1.46)

Then, the results of Theorem
1.6
apply to all solutions of the
initial-transmission problems (
1.41
)-(
1.43
), and
(
1.44
)-(
1.46
). Note, however, that with the exception of the
generic case, in these two situations it is still necessary to verify
that the condition
P_{-}\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}P_{-}
holds.

Remark 1.11

Theorem
1.6
allows us to construct the
inverse wave operators for (
1.24
)-(
1.26
) as
follows. Let
v_{0}
satisfy the assumptions of Theorem
1.6
. Let us
denote

u_{\pm\infty,{\mathbb{R}}}:=\mathbf{F}_{0,{\mathbb{R}}}^{\dagger}w_{\pm\infty,{\mathbb{R}}}.

Then, by (
1.34
)

\lim_{t\rightarrow\pm\infty}\left\|v_{\pm}(t)-e^{-itH_{0,{\mathbb{R}}}}u_{\pm\infty,{\mathbb{R}}}\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})}=0,

and we can define the inverse wave operators
W_{\pm,{\mathbb{R}}}^{-1}
as
follows,

W_{\pm,{\mathbb{R}}}^{-1}v_{0}:=u_{\pm\infty,{\mathbb{R}}}.

With the methods of this paper we can also construct the direct wave operators
and the scattering matrix for small solutions. We will consider these problems
in a different publication.

1.1.4
Comments on the proof.

Our approach is based on the factorization technique. For this purpose, we
find it convenient to go to an interaction representation in momentum space
(Fourier space). We define,

w_{\pm}(k):=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{l}w_{\pm}(k):=(\mathbf{F}e^{itH}u_{\pm})(k),\qquad k\geq 0,\\
\\
w_{\pm}(k)=S(k)w_{\pm}(-k),\qquad k\leq 0,\end{array}\right.

where
\mathbf{F}:=\mathbf{F}^{+},
and
\mathbf{F}^{+}
is the generalized
Fourier map for
H_{A,B,V}
defined in (
A.21
). We find it convenient
to extend
w_{\pm}
to negative
k
with the symmetry
w_{\pm}(k)=S(k)w_{\pm}(-k),k\in{\mathbb{R}}.
We then factorize the solutions
u_{\pm}
to
(
1.1
)- (
1.3
) as follows,

u_{\pm}\left(t\right)=M{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\mathcal{W}\left(t\right)w_{\pm}\left(t\right),

(1.47)

where
{\mathcal{W}}\left(t\right)
is defined in (
A.126
). Similar
factorizations were previously used in
[
47
,
48
]
. Then, if
H_{A,B,V}
does not have negative eigenvalues, we obtain the following equation for the
new variable
w_{\pm},

i\partial_{t}w_{\pm}=\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\left(t\right)\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|\left(\mathcal{W}\left(t\right)w\right)_{1}\right|,...,\left|\left(\mathcal{W}\left(t\right)w\right)_{n}\right|\right)\mathcal{W}\left(t\right)w\right),

(1.48)

with
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(t)
defined in (
A.137
). We then transfer the
problem to the study of
w_{\pm}.
The advantage of this new equation is the
isolation of the nonlinear interaction. However, the price to pay for this
change of variables is dealing with
\mathcal{W}\left(t\right),
and

\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\left(t\right).
In
[
47
,
48
]
, the

L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
norm of
w_{\pm}
is controlled in terms of the

H^{1}({\mathbb{R}})
norm of
w_{\pm}.
For generic potentials or exceptional
potentials with additional symmetry assumptions, it is possible to control
this
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}})
norm without gaining any time growth. It is seems
problematic to apply the same strategy in the case of general exceptional
potentials since one can only prove
\left\|\partial_{k}w\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})}\lesssim\sqrt{|t|}
by the approach of
[
47
,
48
]
. In this paper we use the time-oscillations of
\mathcal{W}\left(t\right)
and of
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\left(t\right)
in equation
(
1.48
) in order to overcome this difficulty. In this way we manage to
bound uniformly in time the
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}})
norm of
w_{\pm}.
We also
control the second derivative
\partial_{k}^{2}w_{\pm},
and prove that the

H^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
norm of
w_{\pm}
can grow in time at most as

\sqrt{|t|}.
Moreover, we prove that also the
L_{2}^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
norm
of
w_{\pm}
can as most grow as
\sqrt{|t|}.
See Remark
2.3

1.2
Outline of the paper.

The remaining sections of the paper are organized as follows. Section
2

is devoted to the proof of our main result, Theorem
1.3
. We
first prove in Theorem
2.1
, in Subsection
2.1
, the
existence of solutions that are local in time, by means of a contraction
mapping argument. The difficulty here consists in enforcing the boundary
condition in our contraction mapping argument. Then, in
Subsection
2.2
we show that the proof of Theorem
1.3

can equivalently be formulated in our interaction representation in Fourier
(momentum) space, and prove Theorem
1.3
by means of the

a priori
estimates that we give in Lemma
2.2
. These
estimates allow us to prove that the local solutions are actually global in
time, and to study their behaviour for large times. The
a priori

estimates in Lemma
2.2
are our core estimates, and we prove
them in Section
4
. However, before that, in
Section
3
, we prove several auxiliary estimates that we need. The
results of this section relay heavily on Appendix
A
. The proof of
Lemma
2.2
in Section
4
is divided in several
subsections. In subsection
4.1
we prove (
2.87
) and we show
that (
2.88
) and (
2.90
) follow, respectively, from (
2.89
)
and (
2.91
). In Subsection
4.2
we reduce the proof of
(
2.89
) and (
2.91
) to prove Lemmas
4.1
,
4.2
, and
4.3
. Actually, in order to control the derivatives of
the nonlinear interaction in the right-hand side of (
1.48
), we decompose
it into the "perturbed" part
\theta_{\pm}
and the "free" part

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}\left(\tau\right)f_{1,\pm}^{\operatorname*{fr}}
.
Sections
4.3
and
4.4
are devoted, respectively, to the
control of the derivatives of
\theta_{\pm},
and of
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}\left(\tau\right)f_{1,\pm}^{\operatorname*{fr}}.
Again, for these
purposes we relay heavily in the results in Appendix
A
. Finally, in
Appendix
A
, we present results on the spectral and the scattering
theory for the matrix Schrödinger equation on the half-line, with the
general selfadjoint boundary condition, that we need, and that are also of
independent interest.

2
Proof of Theorem
1.3

2.1
Local solutions

We first prove that the problem
(
1.1
)-(
1.3
) has local solutions.

Theorem 2.1

Suppose that assumption (
1.13
)
holds, that the potential
V
is selfadjoint, that it belongs to
L_{4}^{1}({\mathbb{R}})
and that it admits a regular decomposition (see Definition

1.1
). Further, assume that the boundary matrices
A,B
fulfill
(
1.5
) and (
1.6
), and that the Hamiltonian
H_{A,B,V}
has no
negative eigenvalues. Let the initial data
u_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap H^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
satisfy the boundary
condition
-B^{\dagger}u_{0}\left(0\right)+A^{\dagger}u_{0}^{\prime}\left(0\right)=0
. Then, for some
T>0,
(
1.1
)-(
1.3
)
has a unique solution,

u_{+}\in C\left(\left[0,T\right];H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\right)\cap C^{1}\left([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\right),

and a unique solution

u_{-}\in C\left(\left[-T,0\right];H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\right)\cap C^{1}\left([-T,0];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\right).

Furthermore, for any
\varepsilon>0
there is a positive number

T_{\varepsilon}
such that if the initial data,
u(0),
satisfies,

\left\|u_{0}\right\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\left\|u_{0}\right\|_{H^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\left\|u_{0}\right\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|u_{0}\|_{H_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq\varepsilon,

(2.1)

the existence time of the solutions,
u_{\pm}(t),
satisfies
T\geq T_{\varepsilon},
respectively,
-T\leq-T_{\varepsilon}.
Moreover,
u_{\pm}(t)
can be extended on a maximal existence interval
[0,T_{+,\max}),

respectively,
(T_{-,\max},0],

u_{+}\in{C}\left(\left[0,T_{+,\max}\right);H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\right)\cap{C}^{1}\left([0,T_{+,\max}];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\right),

and

u_{-}\in{C}\left(\left(T_{-,\max},0\right];H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\right)\cap{C}^{1}\left(\left(T_{-,\max},0\right];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\right),

Moreover if for some constant
C

\left\|u_{+}(t)\right\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|u_{+}(t)\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C,\qquad t\in[0,T_{+,\max}),

(2.2)

then,
T_{+,\max}=\infty.
Further, if for some constant
C

\left\|u_{-}(t)\right\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|u_{-}(t)\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C,\qquad t\in(T_{-,\max},0],

(2.3)

then,
T_{-,\max}=-\infty.

Proof.
We depart from the integral equation corresponding to (
1.1
), that is

u\left(t\right)=\mathcal{U}\left(t\right)u_{0}-i\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{U}\left(t-\tau\right)\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right)d\tau,

(2.4)

with
\mathcal{U}(t):=e^{-itH_{A,B,V}}.
For
T>0,
we denote

I_{+,T}:=[0,T],\qquad I_{-,T}:=[-T,0].

We denote by
G_{\pm,T}
the Banach space,
C(I_{\pm,T};H^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{+})\cap L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}))\cap C^{1}(I_{\pm,T};L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})),
with norm,

\|u\|_{G_{\pm,T}}:=\sup_{t\in I_{\pm,T}}\left(\|u(t)\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|u(t)\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|\partial_{t}u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right).

(2.5)

For
u_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap H^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
that satisfies the boundary condition
-B^{\dagger}u_{0}(0)+A^{\dagger}\partial_{x}u_{0}(0)=0,
we define,

\mathcal{Q}_{\pm}(u):=\mathcal{U}(t)u_{0}-i\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{U}\left(t-\tau\right)\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right)d\tau,\qquad u\in G_{\pm,T}.

(2.6)

We will prove that
\mathcal{Q}_{\pm}
has a unique fixed point in
G_{\pm,T}

by means of the contraction mapping theorem. This unique fixed point is the
solution to the integral equation (
2.4
). Assume that
u\in G_{\pm,T}.

Let
\theta\in C^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
be such that

\theta\left(k\right)=1,
for
\left|k\right|\leq 1,
and

\theta\left(k\right)=0,

\left|k\right|\geq 2.
Since
H
has
no eigenvalues,
P_{c}(H)=I.
Then, using (
A.118
)
with
P_{c}(H)=I,
we write

\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{U}\left(-\tau\right)\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right)d\tau=l_{1}+l_{2},

(2.7)

where

\begin{array}[c]{l}l_{1}=\int_{0}^{t}\,d\tau\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dk\left[f\left(k,x\right)+f\left(-k,x\right)S(-k)\right](1-\theta\left(k\right))e^{i\tau k^{2}}\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right),\end{array}

(2.8)

and

\begin{array}[c]{l}l_{2}=\int_{0}^{t}\,d\tau\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dk\left[f\left(k,x\right)+f\left(-k,x\right)S(-k)\right]\theta\left(k\right)e^{i\tau k^{2}}\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right)=\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}_{2},\end{array}

(2.9)

where,

\tilde{l}_{2}:=\int_{0}^{t}\,d\tau\theta\left(k\right)<k>^{2}e^{i\tau k^{2}}\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right).

(2.10)

Using that
e^{i\tau k^{2}}=\left(ik^{2}\right)^{-1}\partial_{\tau}e^{i\tau k^{2}}
and integrating by parts with respect to
\tau
in
l_{1}

we have,

l_{1}=l_{11}+l_{12}+l_{13},

(2.11)

where

\begin{array}[c]{l}l_{11}=-i\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dk\left[f\left(k,x\right)+f\left(-k,x\right)S(-k)\right](1-\theta\left(k\right))(k^{2})^{-1}e^{itk^{2}}\\
\\
\left(\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(t\right)-\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(0\right)\right)=\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}_{11}(k),\end{array}

(2.12)

with

\tilde{l}_{11}:=-i(1-\theta\left(k\right))<k>^{2}\,k^{-2}e^{itk^{2}}\left(\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(t\right)-\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(0\right)\right).

(2.13)

Moreover,

\begin{array}[c]{l}l_{12}=-i\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dk\left[f\left(k,x\right)+f\left(-k,x\right)S(-k)\right](1-\theta\left(k\right))(k^{2})^{-1}(e^{itk^{2}}-1)\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(0\right)\\
\\
=\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}_{12}(k),\end{array}

(2.14)

with

\tilde{l}_{12}:=-i(1-\theta\left(k\right))k^{-2}\,<k>^{2}(e^{itk^{2}}-1)\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(0\right).

(2.15)

Further,

\begin{array}[c]{l}l_{13}=i\int_{0}^{t}\,d\tau\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dk\left[f\left(k,x\right)+f\left(-k,x\right)S(-k)\right](1-\theta\left(k\right))(k^{2})^{-1}e^{i\tau k^{2}}\mathbf{F}\partial_{\tau}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right)\\
=\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}_{13},\end{array}

(2.16)

where,

\tilde{l}_{13}:=i\int_{0}^{t}\,d\tau\,(1-\theta\left(k\right))k^{-2}<k>^{2}\,e^{i\tau k^{2}}\mathbf{F}\partial_{\tau}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right).

(2.17)

Using (
1.13
) and Sobolev’s inequality we estimate

\begin{array}[c]{l}\left\|\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(t\right)-\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(0\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq\\
\\
C|t|\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\left[\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right].\end{array}

(2.18)

Then, by (
2.13
), (
2.18
), (
A.18
), and
(
A.22
), recalling that as
H
has no eigenvalues,
E(\mathbb{R}^{+};H)=I,

\|\tilde{l}_{11}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C|t|\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(2.19)

By (
A.21
), since
k\,e^{\pm ikx}=\mp i\partial_{x}e^{\pm ikx},

f(k,x)=e^{ikx}m(k,x),
and integrating by parts,

k\left(\mathbf{F}\psi\right)\left(k\right)=a_{1}\left(\psi\right)(k)+a_{2}\left(\psi\right)(k),

(2.20)

where

a_{1}(\psi)(k):=i\left[-m(k,0)^{\dagger}+S(k)^{\dagger}m(-k,0)^{\dagger}\right]\psi(0),

(2.21)

and

a_{2}(\psi_{2})=i\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(-e^{ikx}\partial_{x}[m(k,x)^{\dagger}\psi(x)]+e^{-ikx}S(-k)^{\dagger}\partial_{x}\left[m(-k,x)^{\dagger}\psi(x)\right]\right)dx.

(2.22)

Further, by (
A.18
), (
A.30
), (
A.32
), Parseval’s
identity, and Sobolev’s inequality we show that

\left\|a_{1}(\psi)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\left\|a_{2}(\psi)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left\|\psi\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(2.23)

Using this estimate, together with (
1.13
), (
2.20
),
and Sobolev’s inequality, we show that

k\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(0\right)=a_{1}\left((\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u)(0)\right)+a_{2}\left((\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u)(0)\right),

(2.24)

with

\left\|a_{1}\left((\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u)(0)\right)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\left\|a_{2}\left((\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u)(0)\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left\|u(0)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha+1}.

(2.25)

Then, since
\left|e^{-itk^{2}}-1\right|\leq C\min\{1,|t|k^{2}\},
we get

\displaystyle\|\tilde{l}_{12}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}=\left\|\left\langle k\right\rangle^{2}\left(1-\theta\left(k\right)\right)k^{-2}\left(e^{itk^{2}}-1\right)\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(0\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1}\left|e^{-itk^{2}}-1\right|^{1/8}a_{1}\left((\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u)(0)\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+

\displaystyle C\left\|\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1}\left|e^{-itk^{2}}-1\right|^{1/2}a_{2}\left((\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u)(0)\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}

\displaystyle\leq C|t|^{1/8}\left\|\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle^{-3/4}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\left\|a_{1}\left((\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u)(0)\right)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+

\displaystyle C\sqrt{|t|}\left\|a_{2}\left((\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u)(0)\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left(|t|^{1/8}+\sqrt{|t|}\right)\left\|u(0)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha+1}.

(2.26)

Next, we calculate

\partial_{t}\mathcal{N}\left(\left|\left(g\right)_{1}\right|,...,\left|\left(g\right)_{n}\right|\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(E_{1}^{\left(j\right)}\left(g\right)\partial_{t}\left(g\right)_{j}+E_{2}^{\left(j\right)}\left(g\right)\partial_{t}\overline{\left(g\right)_{j}}\right),

(2.27)

where we denote

E_{1}^{\left(j\right)}\left(g\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{j}\mathcal{N}\right)\left(\left|\left(g\right)_{1}\right|,...,\left|\left(g\right)_{n}\right|\right)\frac{\overline{\left(g\right)_{j}}}{\left|\left(g\right)_{j}\right|},

and

E_{2}^{\left(j\right)}\left(g\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{j}\mathcal{N}\right)\left(\left|\left(g\right)_{1}\right|,...,\left|\left(g\right)_{n}\right|\right)\frac{\left(g\right)_{j}}{{\left|\left(g\right)_{j}\right|}}.

Then, via (
1.13
) and Sobolev’s inequality, we estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{\partial}_{\tau}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|g_{1}\right|,...,\left|g_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(E_{1}^{\left(j\right)}\left(g\right)\partial_{\tau}g_{j}+E_{2}^{\left(j\right)}\left(g\right)\partial_{\tau}\overline{g_{j}}\right)u+\mathcal{N}\left(\left|g_{1}\right|,...,\left|g_{n}\right|\right)\mathbf{\partial}_{\tau}g\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|g\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\left\|\partial_{\tau}g\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(2.28)

and then,
by (
2.17
), (
2.28
) (
A.18
), and (
A.22
), we
get,

\|\tilde{l}_{13}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C|t|\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(2.29)

Moreover, by (
1.13
), (
2.10
), (
A.22
), and
Sobolev’s inequality, we obtain,

\|\tilde{l}_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C|t|\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(2.30)

We define,

\tilde{l}=\tilde{l}_{11}+\tilde{l}_{12}+\tilde{l}_{13}+\tilde{l}_{2}.

(2.31)

Hence, by (
2.7
), (
2.9
), (
2.11
), (
2.12
),
(
2.14
), (
2.16
), (
2.19
), (
2.26
),
(
2.29
) and (
2.30
),

\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{U}\left(-\tau\right)\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right)d\tau=\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-2}\tilde{l},

(2.32)

with

\begin{array}[c]{l}\left\|\tilde{l}\right\|_{L^{{}^{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left(|t|^{1/8}+|t|\right)\sup_{\tau\in[0,T]}\left(\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha+1}+\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right).\end{array}

(2.33)

Since the domain of
H
is contained in
H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
it follows
from (
2.32
), (
2.33
), and (
A.24
),

\begin{array}[c]{l}\|\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{U}\left(t-\tau\right)\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right)d\tau\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq\\
\\
C\left[\|H\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{U}\left(t-\tau\right)\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right)d\tau\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right.+\\
\\
\left.\|\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{U}\left(t-\tau\right)\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right)d\tau\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right]\\
\leq C\left(|t|^{1/8}+|t|\right)\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\left(\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right.\\
\\
\left.+\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right).\end{array}

(2.34)

Next, integration by parts yields

\begin{array}[c]{l}\partial_{t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{U}\left(t-\tau\right)\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right)d\tau=\mathcal{U}(t)\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(0\right)+\\
\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{U}\left(t-\tau\right)\partial_{\tau}\left(\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right)\right)d\tau.\end{array}

(2.35)

Then, using (
2.28
) we get

\begin{array}[c]{l}\left\|\partial_{t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{U}\left(t-\tau\right)\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right)d\tau\ \right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left\|u(0)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\|u(0)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\\
\\
+C|t|\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.\end{array}

(2.36)

By (
2.32
) and (
2.33
)

\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{U}\left(-\tau\right)\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right)d\tau\in D[H],

(2.37)

where
D[H]
denotes the domain of
H.
Then, by (
2.32
), (
2.33
)
and (
A.169
)

\begin{array}[c]{l}\left\|x^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{U}\left(t-\tau\right)\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right)d\tau\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C|t|(1+|t|^{3})\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\left[\left\|\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-2}\tilde{l}(\tau)\right\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right.\\
\\
\left.+\left\|\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-2}\tilde{l}(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\left\|\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-2}\tilde{l}(\tau)\right\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right].\end{array}

(2.38)

By (
2.13
) and (
A.23
)

\begin{array}[c]{l}x^{2}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}_{11}=i\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left[(\partial_{k}^{2}e^{ikx})m(k,x)+(\partial_{k}^{2}e^{-ikx})m(-k,x)S(-k)\right](1-\theta\left(k\right))\,k^{-2}e^{itk^{2}}\\
\\
\left(\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(t\right)-\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(0\right)\right)\,dk.\end{array}

(2.39)

Integrating by parts in (
2.39
), and using (
A.18
),
(
A.30
), (
A.31
), (
A.34
),
(
A.62
),(
A.63
), and Parseval’s identity
we obtain,

\begin{array}[c]{l}\|x^{2}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}_{11}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C(1+t^{2})\left[\|\left(\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(t\right)-\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(0\right)\right)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right.\\
\\
\left.+\|\partial_{k}\left(\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(t\right)-\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(0\right)\right)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\right.\\
\\
\left.\|\partial_{k}^{2}\left(\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(t\right)-\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(0\right)\right)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right].\end{array}

(2.40)

Moreover, by (
1.13
), (
A.22
) and Sobolev’s inequality,

\begin{array}[c]{l}\|\left(\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(t\right)-\right.\\
\\
\left.\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(0\right)\right)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C|t|\sup_{\tau\in\ [0,t]}\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.\end{array}

(2.41)

Further, by (
1.13
), (
A.86
), and Sobolev’s
inequality,

\begin{array}[c]{l}\|\partial_{k}\left(\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(t\right)-\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(0\right)\right)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\\
\\
\leq C|t|\sup_{\tau\in[0,T]}\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha-1}\|u(\tau)\|_{L_{1}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.\end{array}

(2.42)

Moreover, by (
1.13
), (
A.87
), and Sobolev’s
inequality,

\begin{array}[c]{l}\|\partial_{k}^{2}\left(\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(t\right)-\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(0\right)\right)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq\\
\\
C|t|\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha-1}\|u(\tau)\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.\end{array}

(2.43)

By (
2.40
), (
2.41
), (
2.42
) and (
2.43
),

\begin{array}[c]{l}\|x^{2}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}_{11}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C(1+t^{2})\,|t|\sup_{\tau\in[0,T]}\left[\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right.\\
\\
\left.+\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha-1}\|u(\tau)\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right].\end{array}

(2.44)

As in the proof of (
2.44
) we show,

\|x^{2}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}_{12}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C|t|(1+|t|)\left\|u(0)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\left\|u(0)\right\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})},

(2.45)

and,

\begin{array}[c]{l}\|x^{2}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}_{13}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C(1+t^{2})\,|t|\sup_{\tau\in[0,T]}\left[\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right.\\
\\
+\left.\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha-1}\|u(\tau)\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right].\end{array}

(2.46)

Since
H
has no eigenvalues,
P_{c}(H)=I,
and then, by (
A.25
),

\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\mathbf{F}=I.
Hence, it follows from (
2.10
),

\begin{array}[c]{l}x^{2}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}_{2}=x^{2}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\int_{0}^{t}\,d\tau\left[\theta\left(k\right)e^{i\tau k^{2}}-1\right]\mathbf{F}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right)+\\
\\
x^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\,d\tau\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right).\end{array}

(2.47)

Then, as in the proof of (
2.44
) we obtain,

\begin{array}[c]{l}\|x^{2}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C(1+t^{2})\,|t|\sup_{\tau\in[0,T]}\|u(\tau)\|_{H_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\|u(\tau)\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.\end{array}

(2.48)

By (
2.31
), (
2.44
), (
2.45
), (
2.46
) and
(
2.48
)

\begin{array}[c]{l}\|x^{2}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C|t|(1+t^{2})\sup_{\tau\in[0,T]}\left[\left(\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\right.\right.\\
\\
\left.\left.+\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha-1}\|u(\tau)\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right)\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right.\\
\\
\left.+\|u(\tau)\|_{H_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\|u(\tau)\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right].\end{array}

(2.49)

As in the proof of (
2.49
), we show,

\|\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C|t|\sup_{\tau\in[0,T]}\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\left[\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|u(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right].

(2.50)

By (
2.49
) and (
2.50
)

\begin{array}[c]{l}\|x^{2}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C|t|(1+t^{2})\sup_{\tau\in[0,T]}\left[\left(\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\right.\right.\\
\\
\left.\left.+\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha-1}\|u(\tau)\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right)\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right.\\
\\
\left.+\|u(\tau)\|_{H_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\|u(\tau)\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right].\end{array}

(2.51)

Moreover, as in the proof of (
2.49
) we get,

\begin{array}[c]{l}\|x\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C|t|(1+|t|)\sup_{\tau\in[0,T]}\left[\left(\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\right.\right.\\
\\
\left.\left.+\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha-1}\|u(\tau)\|_{L_{1}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right)\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right.\left.+\|u(\tau)\|_{H_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\|u(\tau)\|_{L_{1}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right].\end{array}

(2.52)

Further, as in the proof of (
2.49
), and using also (
A.32
) we
prove,

\|\partial_{x}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C(\sqrt{|t|}+|t|)\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\,\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\left[\|u(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right],

(2.53)

and, furthermore, using also (
A.33
) we obtain,

\begin{array}[c]{l}\|x\partial_{x}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C(\sqrt{|t|}+|t|)\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\,\left[\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\left(\|u(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right)\right.\\
\\
\left.+\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha-1}\|u(\tau)\|_{L_{1}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\left(\|u(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right)\right].\end{array}

(2.54)

By (
2.50
), (
2.52
), (
2.53
) and
(
2.54
),

\begin{array}[c]{l}\|\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}\|_{H^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C(\sqrt{|t|}+|t|)\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\,\left[\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\left(\|u(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right)\right.\left.+\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha-1}\|u(\tau)\|_{L_{1}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\left(\|u(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right)\right].\end{array}

(2.55)

Moreover, as
D[H]\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
it follows from
(
2.33
) and (
A.24
),

\begin{array}[c]{l}\|\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left[\|H\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{<k>^{2}}\tilde{l}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right]\leq\\
\\
C\left(t|^{1/8}+|t|\right)\sup_{\tau\in[0,T]}\left(\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha+1}\right.\left.+\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right).\end{array}

(2.56)

By (
2.38
), (
2.51
), (
2.55
), and
(
2.56
)

\begin{array}[c]{l}\left\|\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{U}\left(t-\tau\right)\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right)d\tau\right\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C|t|(|t|^{1/8}+|t|^{5})\sup_{\tau\in[0,T]}\left[\left(\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\right.\right.\\
\\
\left.\left.+\left\|u(\tau)\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha-1}\|u(\tau)\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right)\left\|\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right.\\
\left.+\|u(\tau)\|_{H_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\|u(\tau\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|u(\tau)\|_{H_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha+1}\right].\end{array}

(2.57)

Since by (
A.13
)
u_{0}
belongs to the domain of
H,
and as the
domain of
H
is contained in
H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),

\|\mathcal{U}(t)u_{0}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left[\|H\mathcal{U}(t)u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|\mathcal{U}(t)u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right]\leq C\|u_{0}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})},

(2.58)

and, further, by (
A.169
)

\|\mathcal{U}(t)u_{0}\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C(1+|t|^{3})\left(\|u_{0}\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|u_{0}\|_{H^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|u_{0}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right).

(2.59)

Moreover,

\|\partial_{t}\mathcal{U}(t)u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}=\|\mathcal{U}(t)Hu_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|u_{0}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(2.60)

Then by (
2.6
), (
2.34
), (
2.57
), (
2.58
),
(
2.59
), and (
2.60
)

\begin{array}[c]{l}\|\mathcal{Q}_{\pm}(u)\|_{G_{\pm,T}}\leq C\left[(1+T^{3})\left(\|u_{0}\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|u_{0}\|_{H^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|u_{0}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right)\right.\\
\\
\left.+\|u_{0}\|_{H_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+(1+T)(T^{1/8}+T^{5})\|u\|_{G_{\pm,T}}^{\alpha+1}\right].\end{array}

(2.61)

Denote,

\mathcal{M}:=2C\left[\|u_{0}\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|u_{0}\|_{H^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|u_{0}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right.\\
\\
\left.+\|u_{0}\|_{H_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right].

(2.62)

Let
G_{\pm,T}(\mathcal{M})
be the ball of center zero and radius

\mathcal{M}
in
G_{\pm,T}.
Then, by (
2.61
) we can take
T
so
small that,

\|\mathcal{Q}_{\pm}(u)\|_{G_{\pm,T}}\leq\mathcal{M},\qquad u\in G_{\pm,T}(\mathcal{M}).

(2.63)

By a slight modification of the arguments that we used to prove
(
2.63
) we obtain that we can take
T
so that also,

\|\mathcal{Q}_{\pm}(u)-\mathcal{Q}_{\pm}(v)\|_{G_{\pm,T}}<\|u-v\|_{G_{\pm,T}},\qquad u,v\in G_{\pm,T}(\mathcal{M}).

(2.64)

Then, by the contraction mapping theorem (see Theorem V.18 in page 151 of

[
53
]
), the map
\mathcal{Q}_{\pm}
has a unique fixed point,
u_{\pm}(t)
in
G_{I_{\pm,T}}(\mathcal{M})
that is a solution to (
2.4
).
Moreover, as
u_{0}\in D[H],
it follows from (
2.37
) that
u_{\pm}(t)\in D[H],
and then,
u_{\pm}(t)
satisfies the boundary condition

-B^{\dagger}u(t,0)+A^{\dagger}(\partial_{x}u)(t,0)=0.

Further, for any initial state
u_{0}
that satisfies (
2.1
) we have
that,

\mathcal{M}\leq C(\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{\alpha+1}).

(2.65)

Then, by (
2.61
) we can take a fixed
T_{\varepsilon}
such that,

\|\mathcal{Q}_{\pm}(u)\|_{G_{\pm,T_{\varepsilon}}}\leq\mathcal{M},\qquad u\in G_{\pm,T_{\varepsilon}}(\mathcal{M})

(2.66)

for all initial data
u_{0}
that satisfy (
2.1
). In a similar way, we
prove that,

\|\mathcal{Q}_{\pm}(u)-\mathcal{Q}_{\pm}(v)\|_{G_{\pm,T_{\varepsilon}}}<\|u-v\|_{G_{\pm,T_{\varepsilon}}},\qquad u,v\in G_{\pm,T_{\varepsilon}}(\mathcal{M}),

(2.67)

for all initial data
u_{0}
that satisfy (
2.1
). Hence, the existence
time,
T,
of all the solutions
u_{\pm}(t)
with initial data,
u_{0},
that
satisfy (
2.1
) is bounded below by
T_{\varepsilon},
i.e.,
T\geq T_{\varepsilon}.
Moreover, suppose that (
2.2
), respectively
(
2.3
) holds and that
T_{+,\mathrm{max}}<\infty,
respectively,

T_{-,\mathrm{max}}<\infty.
Hence,

\|u_{\pm}(t)\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|u_{\pm}(t)\|_{L_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C,\qquad t\in[0,T_{+,\mathrm{max}}),\,\text{respectively, }\,t\in(T_{-,\text{max}},0].

(2.68)

Further, taking the derivative with respect to
t
of both sides of
(
2.4
) we obtain that (
1.1
) holds, and then,

\|\partial_{t}u_{\pm}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|u_{\pm}(t)\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\left(1+\|u_{\pm}(t)\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{\alpha}\right)\leq C,

(2.69)

where we used (
1.13
) and Sobolev’s inequality. Then, by
(
2.61
) we can take the limit as
t\rightarrow T_{+,\mathrm{max}}
in
both sides of (
2.4
) and define,

u_{\pm}(T_{\pm,\mathrm{max}})=\lim_{T\rightarrow T_{\pm,\mathrm{max}}}u_{\pm}(t)=\mathcal{U}\left(T_{\pm,\mathrm{max}}\right)u_{0}-i\int_{0}^{T_{\pm,\mathrm{max}}}\mathcal{U}\left(T_{\pm,\mathrm{max}}-\tau\right)\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|(u_{\pm})_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|(u_{\pm})_{n}\right|\right)u_{\pm}\right)\left(\tau\right)d\tau.

(2.70)

Moreover, by (
2.32
), (
2.34
), (
2.51
),
(
2.52
), (
2.53
), (
2.54
), (
2.58
),
(
2.70
), (
A.167
), (
A.168
), and (
A.170
),

\begin{array}[c]{l}\|u_{\pm}(T_{\pm,\mathrm{max}})\|_{H^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C(1+T_{\pm,\mathrm{max}}^{2})\left[\|u(0)\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|u(0)\|_{H^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|u_{0}\|_{L_{1}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right.\\
\\
\left.+C(T_{\pm,\mathrm{max}}^{1/8}+T_{\pm,\mathrm{max}}^{5})\|u_{\pm}\|_{G_{T_{\pm,\mathrm{max}}}}^{\alpha+1}\right].\end{array}

(2.71)

Then, we can solve the integral equation

u\left(t\right)=\mathcal{U}\left(t-T_{\pm,\mathrm{max}}\right)u_{\pm}(T_{\pm,\max})-i\int_{T_{\pm,\mathrm{max}}}^{t}\mathcal{U}\left(t-\tau\right)\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|u_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|u_{n}\right|\right)u\right)\left(\tau\right)d\tau,

in an interval
t\in[T_{+,\mathrm{max}},T_{1}],
respectively,

[T_{2},T_{-,\mathrm{max}}],
and extend the solution
u_{\pm}(t),
to the
interval
[0,T_{1}],T_{1}>T_{\mathrm{max}}
, respectively,
[T_{2},T_{-,\mathrm{max}}],
in contradiction with the definition of

T_{+,\mathrm{max}},
respectively,
T_{-,\mathrm{max}}.
This completes the
proof of the theorem.

2.2
Global solutions

In this subsection we prove that for small initial data we can
extend the local solutions given by Theorem
2.1
into global
solutions, i.e. solutions with
T_{\pm,\mathrm{max}}=\infty.
For this
purpose, we find it convenient to go to a interaction representation in
momentum space (Fourier space). We suppose that
H=H_{A,B,V}
does not have
negative eigenvalues. Then, by Theorem
A.4

H
is absolutely
continuous,
P_{c}(H)=I,
and the generalized Fourier maps
\mathbf{F}^{\pm}

are unitary, and in particular
\left(\mathbf{F}^{\pm}\right)^{\dagger}\mathbf{F}^{\pm}=\mathbf{F}^{\pm}\left(\mathbf{F}^{\pm}\right)^{\dagger}=I.
Below we use
\mathbf{F}^{+},
but we could use
\mathbf{F}^{-}
as
well. For simplicity we denote
\mathbf{F}:=\mathbf{F}^{+}.
Let
u_{\pm}
be
the local solution to (
1.1
) given by Theorem
2.1
. We define,

w_{\pm}(k):=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{l}w_{\pm}(k):=(\mathbf{F}e^{itH}u_{\pm})(k),\qquad k\geq 0\\
\\
w_{\pm}(k)=S(k)w_{\pm}(-k),\qquad k\leq 0.\end{array}\right.

(2.72)

Recall that
u_{\pm}\in D[H]\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).
Then,

\|e^{itH}u_{\pm}(t)\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left(\|e^{itH}Hu_{\pm}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|e^{itH}u_{\pm}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right)\leq C\|u_{\pm}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(2.73)

Further, by (
2.4
), (
2.32
), and (
2.51
)

\|e^{itH}u_{\pm}(t)\|_{L^{2}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left[\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+T(1+T^{2})\|u_{\pm}\|_{G_{\pm,T}}^{\alpha+1}\right].

(2.74)

Hence, by (
2.73
), (
2.74
) and Lemma
A.11
,

\begin{array}[c]{l}w_{+}\in C([0,T];H^{2}({\mathbb{R}})\cap L^{2}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})),\\
\\
w_{-}\in C([-T,0];H^{2}({\mathbb{R}})\cap L^{2}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})).\end{array}

(2.75)

Further, by (
2.72
),

\partial_{t}w_{\pm}(t,k)=\mathbf{F}\left(ie^{itH}Hu_{\pm}(t)+e^{itH}\partial_{t}u_{\pm}(t)\right)(k),\qquad k\geq 0.

(2.76)

Then, by (
2.72
) and (
2.76
)

w_{+}\in C^{1}([0,T];L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})),\qquad w_{-}\in C^{1}([-T,0];L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})).

(2.77)

In order to derive the equation for
w_{\pm}\left(t\right),
we apply the
operator
\mathbf{F}e^{itH}
to equation (
1.1
). Since
e^{-itH}
is the
linear evolution group, we find

i\partial_{t}\left(\mathbf{F}e^{itH}u_{\pm}\right)=\mathbf{F}e^{itH}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|(u_{\pm})_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|(u_{\pm})_{n}\right|\right)u_{\pm}\right).

(2.78)

Let us denote by
\tilde{w}_{\pm}(t,k)
the restriction of
w_{\pm}(t,k)
to

k\in\mathbb{R}^{+}.
Then, since by (
2.72
), we have,

u_{\pm}\left(t\right)=e^{-itH}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\tilde{w}_{\pm},

(2.79)

substituting (
2.79
) into (
2.78
) we obtain the equation for

\tilde{w}_{\pm}

i\partial_{t}\tilde{w}_{\pm}=\mathbf{F}e^{itH}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|\left(e^{-itH}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\tilde{w}_{\pm}\right)_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|\left(e^{-itH}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\tilde{w}_{\pm}\right)_{n}\right|\right)e^{-itH}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\tilde{w}_{\pm}\right).

(2.80)

By (
A.130
) with
P_{c}(H)=I,
and (
A.133
) we get,

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}e^{itH}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left|\left(e^{-itH}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\tilde{w}_{\pm}\right)_{1}\right|,\dots,\left|\left(e^{-itH}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\tilde{w}_{\pm}\right)_{n}\right|\right)e^{-itH}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\tilde{w}_{\pm}\right)
