).

Appendix A
Appendix. The linear matrix Schrödinger equation on the half
line

In this appendix we present results on the spectral and the
scattering theory for the matrix Schrödinger equation on the half line, with
the general selfadjoint boundary condition, that we need. Some of the results
that we give here are borrowed from
[
6
]
, and others are new.

We consider the matrix Schrödinger equation on the half line given by,

-\psi^{\prime\prime}+V\left(x\right)\psi=k^{2}\psi,\text{ }x\in\mathbb{R}^{+}.

(A.1)

The prime denotes the derivative with respect to the spacial coordinate
x,

the scalar
k^{2}
is the complex-valued spectral parameter, the potential
V
is a
n\times n
self-adjoint matrix-valued function that belongs at least
to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).
Later we impose further conditions of
V.

Moreover,
n
is any positive integer. The selfadjointness of
V
means that,

V(x)=V^{\dagger}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}^{+},

(A.2)

where the dagger denotes the matrix adjoint. The wavefunction that appears in
(
A.1
) may be an
n\times n
matrix-valued function or it may be
a column vector with n components. Our interest is to study
(
A.1
) under the general self-adjoint boundary condition at

x=0.
For a discussion of the general self-adjoint boundary condition at
x=0
see Section 2.2 in Chapter 2, and Sections 3.4 and 3.6 in Chapter 3 of

[
6
]
. We find it convenient to use the parametrization of the
general self-adjoint boundary condition at
x=0,
given in
[
6
]
in
terms of two constant
n\times n
matrices
A
and
B
as,

-B^{\dagger}\psi(0)+A^{\dagger}\psi^{\prime}(0)=0,

(A.3)

where
A
and
B
satisfy,

B^{\dagger}A=A^{\dagger}B,

(A.4)

A^{\dagger}A+B^{\dagger}B>0.

(A.5)

We call
A
and
B
the boundary matrices. The condition in (
A.4
) means
that the matrix
A^{\dagger}B
is self-adjoint, and the condition in
(
A.5
) means that the matrix
A^{\dagger}A+B^{\dagger}B
is positive
definite. One can directly verify that the boundary condition
(
A.3
)-(
A.5
) remains invariant if the boundary matrices
A,
and

B
are replaced, respectively, by
AT
and
BT
where
T
is an arbitrary

n\times n
invertible matrix. Actually, it is proved in Proposition 2.2.1 in
page 23 of
[
6
]
that this is the only freedom in the choice of the
matrices
A,B
. In other words, the boundary condition
(
A.3
)-(
A.5
) is uniquely determined by the matrix pair
(A,B)

modulo an invertible matrix
T,
or, equivalently, the boundary condition
(
A.3
)-(
A.5
) determines the matrix pair
(A,B)
modulo
T.
There
is a simpler equivalent form of the boundary condition (
A.3
) where the
matrices
A,B
are diagonal. In Section 3.4 of Chapter three of

[
6
]
, it is given the explicit steps to go from any pair of
matrices
A
and
B
appearing in the selfadjoint boundary condition
(
A.3
), and that satisfy (
A.4
), and (
A.5
) to a pair

\tilde{A}
and
\tilde{B},
given by

\tilde{A}=-\operatorname*{diag}\left\{\sin\theta_{1},...,\sin\theta_{n}\right\},\text{ }\tilde{B}=\operatorname*{diag}\left\{\cos\theta_{1},...,\cos\theta_{n}\right\},

(A.6)

with appropriate real parameters
\theta_{j}\in(0,\pi].
The matrices

\tilde{A},\tilde{B}
satisfy (
A.4
), (
A.5
). In the case of the
matrices
\tilde{A}
,
\tilde{B},
the boundary condition (
A.3
) is
given by

\cos\theta_{j}Y_{j}\left(0\right)+\sin\theta_{j}\frac{d}{dx}Y_{j}\left(0\right)=0,\text{ \ \ }j=1,2,...,n.

(A.7)

The case
\theta_{j}=\pi
corresponds to the Dirichlet boundary condition and
the case
\theta_{j}=\pi/2
corresponds to the Neumann boundary condition. In
the general case, there are
n_{\operatorname*{N}}\leq n
values with

\theta_{j}=\pi/2
and
n_{\operatorname*{D}}\leq n
values with
\theta_{j}=\pi
. Further, there are
n_{\operatorname*{M}}
remaining values, where

n_{\operatorname*{M}}=n-n_{\operatorname*{N}}-n_{\operatorname*{D}}
such
that those
\theta_{j}
-values lie in the interval
(0,\pi/2)
or
(\pi/2,\pi).
They correspond to Robin, or mixed, boundary conditions. It is
proven in Section 3.4 of Chapter three of
[
6
]
, that for any pair
of matrices
(A,B)
that satisfy (
A.4
), and (
A.5
) there is a
pair of matrices
(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})
as in (
A.6
), a unitary
matrix
M
and two invertible matrices
T_{1},T_{2}
such

A=M\,\tilde{A}T_{1}M^{\dagger}T_{2},\quad B=M\,\tilde{B}T_{1}M^{\dagger}T_{2},

(A.8)

and further, the Hamiltonians with the boundary condition given by matrices

A,B
and with the matrices
\tilde{A},\tilde{B},
are unitarily equivalent,
as we will see.

We construct a selfadjoint realization of the matrix Schrödinger operator

-\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}+V(x)
by quadratic forms methods. For the following
discussion see Sections 3.3 and 3.5 of Chapter three in
[
6
]
. Let

\theta_{j}
be as in equations (
A.6
). We denote

{H}_{j}^{(1)}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\mathbb{C}):=W_{1,2}^{\left(0\right)}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\mathbb{C}),\text{ if }\theta_{j}=\pi,\text{ and}\,{H}_{j}^{(1)}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\mathbb{C}):=H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\mathbb{C}),\text{ if }\theta_{j}\neq\pi.

(A.9)

We put

\widetilde{{H}}^{(1)}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\mathbb{C}^{n}):=\oplus_{j=1}^{n}{H}_{j}^{(1)}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\mathbb{C}).

We define

\Theta:=\operatorname*{diag}[\widehat{\cot}\theta_{1},...,\widehat{\cot}\theta_{n}],

where
\widehat{\cot}\theta_{j}=0,
if
\theta_{j}=\pi/2,
or
\theta_{j}=\pi,
and
\widehat{\cot}\theta_{j}=\cot\theta_{j},
if
\theta_{j}\neq\pi/2,\pi.
Suppose that the potential
V
is self-adjoint and that it belongs
to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).

The following quadratic form is closed, symmetric and bounded below,

\begin{array}[c]{l}h\left(Y,Z\right):=\left(\frac{d}{dx}Y,\frac{d}{dx}Z\right)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{C}^{n})}-\left\langle M\Theta M^{{\dagger}}Y\left(0\right),Z\left(0\right)\right\rangle+\left(VY,Z\right)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{C}^{n})},\\
Q\left(h\right):={H}_{A,B}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\mathbb{C}^{n}),\end{array}

(A.10)

where by
Q(h)
we denote the domain of
h
and,

{H}^{1}_{A,B}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\mathbb{C}^{n}):=M\widetilde{{H}}^{(1)}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\mathbb{C}^{n})\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\mathbb{C}^{n}).

(A.11)

For simplicity we will also use the notation,

H^{1}_{A,B}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\equiv H^{1}_{A,B}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\mathbb{C}^{n}).

We denote by
H_{A,B,V}
the selfadjoint bounded below operator associated to

h

[
39
]
. The operator
H_{A,B,V}
is the selfadjoint realization of

\displaystyle-\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}+V\left(x\right)
with the selfadjoint
boundary condition (
A.3
). Actually, it is proven in Proposition 3.5.1
in page 107 of
[
6
]
that the domain of
H_{A,B,V}
is given by,

\begin{array}[c]{l}D[H_{A,B,V}]=\left\{Z\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}):Z^{\prime}\,\text{{is absolutely continuous in any}}\,[0,a]\,\text{{for
all}}\,a>0,\right.\\
\left.-B^{\dagger}Z(0)+A^{\dagger}Z^{\prime}(0)=0,\,\text{{and}}\,-\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}Z(x)+V(x)Z(x)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\right\}.\end{array}

(A.12)

If, moreover
V\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
the
domain of
H_{A,B,V}
can be, equivalently, characterized as follows,

D[H_{A,B,V}]=\left\{Z\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}):-B^{\dagger}Z(0)+A^{\dagger}Z^{\prime}(0)=0\right\}.

(A.13)

When there is no possibility of misunderstanding we will use the notation
H,
i.e.,
H\equiv H_{A,B,V}.
It is proven in Section 3.6 of Chapter three of

[
6
]
that,

H_{A,B,V}=MH_{\tilde{A},\tilde{B},M^{\dagger}VM}M^{\dagger}.

(A.14)

In the next proposition we introduce the Jost solution given in

[
4
]
. See also Sections 3.1 and 3.2 of
[
6
]
.

PROPOSITION A.1

Suppose that the potential
V
is self-adjoint and that it belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).

Then, for each fixed

k\in\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}}\backslash\{0\}
there exists a unique
n\times n
matrix-valued Jost solution
f\left(k,x\right)
to equation
(
A.1
) satisfying the asymptotic condition

f\left(k,x\right)=e^{ikx}\left(I+o\left(1\right)\right),\qquad x\rightarrow+\infty.

(A.15)

Moreover, for any fixed
x\in[0,\infty),

f\left(k,x\right)
is
analytic in
k\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
and continuous in
k\in\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}}\setminus\{0\}
. If, moreover,
V
belongs to
L^{1}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
the Jost solution also exists at
k=0,
and for each fixed
x\in[0,\infty),

f\left(k,x\right)
is continuous in
k\in\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}}.
Furthermore if
V
belongs to
L^{1}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
for
k\in\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}}\backslash\{0\},
the
o(1)
term in (
A.15
) can be replaced by
o\left(\frac{1}{x}\right).

Using the Jost solution and the boundary matrices
A
and
B
satisfying
(
A.4
)-(
A.5
), we construct the Jost matrix
J\left(k\right),

J\left(k\right)=f\left(-k^{*},0\right)^{\dagger}B-f^{\prime}\left(-k^{*},0\right)^{\dagger}A,\text{ \ }k\in\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}},

(A.16)

where the asterisk denotes complex conjugation. For the following result
Theorem 3.8.1 in page 114 of
[
6
]
.

PROPOSITION A.2

Suppose that the potential
V
is self-adjoint and that
it belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).
Then,

J\left(k\right)
is analytic for
k\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
, continuous for

k\in\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}}\setminus\{0\},
and invertible for
k\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}.
Furthermore, if
V
belongs to
L^{1}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
the Jost matrix
J(k)
is continuous at
k=0.

The scattering matrix,
S\left(k\right),
is a
n\times n
matrix-valued
function of
k\in\mathbb{R}
that is given by

S\left(k\right)=-J\left(-k\right)J\left(k\right)^{-1},\text{ }k\in\mathbb{R}\text{.}

(A.17)

In the
exceptional case
where
J(0)
is not invertible the scattering
matrix is defined by (
A.17
) only for
k\neq 0.
However, it
is proved in Theorem 3.8.14 in pages 138-139 of
[
6
]
, that for
self-adjoint potentials
V
that belong to
L_{1}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
the
limit
S(0):=\lim_{k\rightarrow 0}S(k)
exists in the exceptional case and,
moreover, a formula for
S(0)
is given. Furthermore, it is proved in
Proposition 3.8.17 in page 140 of
[
6
]
,

S(-k)=S(k)^{\dagger}=S(k)^{-1},|S(k)|=1,\qquad k\in\mathbb{R}.

(A.18)

It is proved in Theorem 3.10.6 in pages 196-197 of
[
6
]
that the
following limit exist,

S_{\infty}:=\lim_{|k|\rightarrow\infty}S\left(k\right).

(A.19)

Remark A.3

It follows from equation (3.10.37) in page 197 of
[
6
]
that
S_{\infty}=I_{n}
if and only if there are no Dirichlet boundary conditions in the diagonal representation of the boundary matrices given in (
A.6
), (
A.7
), and (
A.8
).
Moreover, if
V\in L^{1}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
by Proposition 3.8.18 in page 142 of
[
6
]
the only two eigenvalues that
S(0)
can have are
+1
and
-1
. We denote by
P_{\pm}
the projectors onto the eigenspaces of
S(0)
corresponding to the eigenvalues
\pm 1,
respectively.
Further, it follows from Theorem 3.8.13 in page 137 of
[
6
]
and Theorem 3.8.14 in pages 138-139 of
[
6
]
, that
S(0)=-I
if and only if we are in the
generic case
where
J(0)
is invertible, and that
S(0)=I
if and only if the geometric multiplicity,
\mu,
of the eigenvalue zero of the zero energy Jost matrix,
J(0),
(see (
A.16
)) is equal to
n.
Moreover, by Remark 3.8.10 in pages 129-130 of
[
6
]
the geometric multiplicity of the eigenvalue zero of
J(0)
is equal to
n
if and only if there are
n
linearly independent bounded solutions to the Schrödinger equation (
A.1
) with zero energy,
k^{2}=0,
that satisfy the boundary condition (
A.3
). This corresponds to the
purely exceptional case
where there are
n
linearly independent half-bound states, or zero-energy resonances.

In terms of the Jost solution
f(k,x)
and the scattering matrix
S(k)
we
construct the physical solution to (
A.1
), see equation (2.2.29)
in page 26 of
[
6
]
,

\Psi\left(k,x\right)=f\left(-k,x\right)+f\left(k,x\right)S\left(k\right),\text{ }k\in\mathbb{R}\text{.}

(A.20)

By Proposition 3.2.12 in page 89 of
[
6
]
the physical solution
satisfies the boundary condition (
A.3
). The physical solution

\Psi(k,x)
is the main input to construct the generalized Fourier maps,

\mathbf{F}^{\pm},
for the absolutely continuous subspace of
H,
that are
defined in equation (4.3.44) in page 284 of
[
6
]
(see also
Proposition 4.3.4 in page 287 of
[
6
]
),

\left(\mathbf{F}^{\pm}Y\right)\left(k\right)=\sqrt{\frac{1}{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\Psi\left(\mp k,x\right)\right)^{\dagger}Y\left(x\right)\,dx,

(A.21)

for
Y\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).

We have (see equation (4.3.46) in page 284 of
[
6
]
),

\left\|\mathbf{F}^{\pm}Y\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}=\left\|E(\mathbb{R}^{+};H)Y\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})},

(A.22)

where, for any Borel set
U
we denote by
E(U;H)
the spectral projector of

H
for the Borel set
U.
Thus, the
\mathbf{F}^{\pm}
extend to bounded
operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
that we also denote by
\mathbf{F}^{\pm}.

The following results on the spectral theory of
H
are proved in Theorem
3.11.1 in pages 199-200, Theorem 4.3.3 in page 284 and Proposition 4.3.4 in
page 287, of
[
6
]
.

THEOREM A.4

Suppose that the potential
V
is self-adjoint, that it belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
and that the constant boundary matrices
A,B
fulfill (
A.4
), (
A.5
). Then, the Hamiltonian
H

has no positive eigenvalues and the negative spectrum of
H
consists of
isolated eigenvalues of multiplicity smaller or equal than
n
, that can
accumulate only at zero. Furthermore,
H
has no singular continuous spectrum
and its absolutely continuous spectrum is given by
[0,\infty)
. The
generalized Fourier maps
\mathbf{F}^{\pm}
are partially isometric with
initial subspace the absolutely continuous subspace of
H
and
final subspace
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
. Moreover, the adjoint operators are given by

\left(\left(\mathbf{F}^{\pm}\right)^{\dagger}Z\right)\left(x\right)=\sqrt{\frac{1}{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Psi\left(\mp k,x\right)Z\left(k\right)\,dk,

(A.23)

for
Z\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).
Furthermore,

\mathbf{F}^{\pm}H\left(\mathbf{F}^{\pm}\right)^{\dagger}=\mathcal{M},

(A.24)

where
\mathcal{M}
is the operator of multiplication by
k^{2}.
If, in
addition,
V\in L^{1}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
zero is not an eigenvalue and the number of
eigenvalues of
H
including multiplicities is finite.

Note that by (
A.22
)
\left(\mathbf{F}^{\pm}\right)^{\dagger}\mathbf{F}^{\pm}
is the orthogonal projector onto the absolutely continuous
subspace of
H,
and as there is no singular continuous spectrum the
absolutely continuous subspace of
H
coincides with the continuous subspace
of
H.
Then,

\left(\mathbf{F}^{\pm}\right)^{\dagger}\mathbf{F}^{\pm}=P_{\operatorname*{c}}(H),

(A.25)

where
P_{\operatorname*{c}}(H)
is the orthogonal projector onto the
continuous subspace of
H.
Moreover, from (
A.24
) and functional
calculus, it follows,

\mathbf{F}^{\pm}e^{-itH}\left(\mathbf{F}^{\pm}\right)^{\ast}=e^{-it\mathcal{M}}.

(A.26)

Assuming faster decay of the potential
V
at infinity we now obtain further
properties of the Jost solution and of the scattering matrix.

Let us denote,

m\left(k,x\right):=e^{-ikx}f\left(k,x\right).

(A.27)

Assuming that
V\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}),
it is proved in the proof of
Proposition 3.2.1 in Chapter 3 of
[
6
]
that the function
m(k,x),

k\in\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}}\setminus\{0\},
is the unique solution of
the following Volterra integral equation (see equation (3.2.9) in page 53 of

[
6
]
),

m\left(k,x\right)=1+\int_{x}^{\infty}D\left(y-x,k\right)V\left(y\right)m\left(k,y\right)dy,

(A.28)

where,

D\left(k,x\right):=\frac{1}{2ik}\left[e^{2ikx}-1\right]=\int_{0}^{x}e^{2iky}\,dy.

(A.29)

We have the following result.

PROPOSITION A.5

Assume that the potential
V
is selfadjoint. Then.

1.

Suppose that
V\in{L}^{1}_{2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
for some
\delta\geq 0.
Then,
for all
k\in\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}}
and
x\geq 0,
we have

\left|m\left(k,x\right)-1\right|\leq C\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-1-\delta},

(A.30)

\left|\partial_{k}m\left(k,x\right)\right|\leq C\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-\delta}.

(A.31)

2.

Assume that
V\in{L}^{1}_{2+\tilde{\delta}}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),\
for some
\tilde{\delta}\geq 0,
and that it admits a regular decomposition. Then, for
all
k\in\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}},
and
x\geq 0,
the following estimates
hold,

\left|\partial_{x}m\left(k,x\right)\right|\leq C\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-2-\hat{\delta}},\hat{\delta}:=\min[\tilde{\delta},\delta],

(A.32)

and

\left|\partial_{k}\partial_{x}m\left(k,x\right)\right|\leq C\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-1-\hat{\delta}},\hat{\delta}:=\min[\tilde{\delta},\delta].

(A.33)

3.

Suppose that
V\in{L}^{1}_{3+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
for some
\delta\geq 0.
Then, for
all
k\in\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}}
and
x\geq 0,
we have

\left|\partial_{k}^{2}m\left(k,x\right)\right|\leq C\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-\delta},

(A.34)

and

\left|\partial_{k}^{2}\partial_{x}m\left(k,x\right)\right|\leq C\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-1-\delta}.\text{ \ }

(A.35)

4.

If
V\in{L}^{1}_{4+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
then, for all
k\in\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}}
and
x\geq 0,
we have

\left|\partial_{k}^{3}m\left(k,x\right)\right|\leq C\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-\delta},

(A.36)

and

\left|\partial_{k}^{3}\partial_{x}m\left(k,x\right)\right|\leq C\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-1-\delta}.\text{ \ }

(A.37)

Proof.
By equations (3.2.13), (3.2.14), (3.2.15) and (3.2.16) in page 54 of

[
6
]
, and since,

|e^{z}-1|\leq C\frac{|z|}{1+|z|},\qquad z\in\mathbf{C},

(A.38)

we have

|m(k,x)|\leq C,\qquad k\in\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}},x\in\mathbb{R}^{+}.

(A.39)

Then, (
A.30
) follows from (
A.28
), the first equality in
(
A.29
), (
A.38
) and (
A.39
). Differentiating with respect to

k
both sides of (
A.28
) we get,

\partial_{k}m(k,x)=\dot{m}_{0}(k,x)+\int_{x}^{\infty}\,dy\,D(k(y-x))\,V(y)\partial_{k}m(k,y),

(A.40)

where, we have defined,

\dot{m}_{0}(k,x):=\frac{1}{2ik^{2}}\,\int_{x}^{\infty}\,dy\,\left[1-e^{2ik(y-x)}+2ik(y-x)e^{2ik(y-x)}\right]\,V(y)\,m(k,y).

(A.41)

We have that,

\left|e^{iz}-iz-1\right|\leq\frac{C|z|^{2}}{1+|z|}.

(A.42)

Moreover, in equation (3.9.15) in page 157 of
[
6
]
it is proved,

\left|\partial_{k}m(k,x)\right|\leq C.

(A.43)

Equation (
A.31
) follows from the first equality in (
A.29
), and
(
A.38
)-(
A.43
). This completes the proof of 1. Let us prove 2.
Taking the derivative of both sides of (
A.28
) with respect to
x
and
using the first equality in (
A.29
) we obtain,

\partial_{x}m(k,x)=-\int_{x}^{\infty}\,dye^{2ik(y-x)}\,V(y)-\int_{x}^{\infty}\,dye^{2ik(y-x)}\,V(y)\,(m(k,y)-1).

(A.44)

Note that

|V(x)|=\left|\int_{x}^{\infty}\,|\partial_{y}V(y)|dy\right|\leq\frac{1}{|x|^{2+\delta}}\,\int_{x}^{\infty}\,|y|^{2+\delta}\,|\partial_{y}V(y)|,\qquad x\geq x_{N}.

(A.45)

Then,

|V(x)|\leq C\frac{1}{<x>^{2+\delta}}.

(A.46)

Using (
A.46
),

e^{2ik(y-x)}=\frac{1}{2ik}\,\partial_{y}e^{2ik(y-x)},

that
<\cdot>^{2+\delta}\,\partial_{x}V\in L^{1}(x_{N},\infty),
and
integrating by parts we prove that,

\left|\int_{x}^{\infty}\,dye^{2ik(y-x)}\,V(y)\right|\leq C\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-2-\delta}.

(A.47)

Hence, (
A.32
) follows from (
A.30
), (
A.44
) and (
A.47
).
Equation (
A.33
) is proved taking the derivative with respect to
k
of
both sides of (
A.44
), using (
A.31
), and arguing as in the proof of
(
A.32
). This finishes the proof of 2. We now proceed to prove 3.
Differentiating both sides of (
A.28
) two times with respect to the
k

variable, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{k}^{2}m\left(k,x\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{x}^{\infty}\partial_{k}^{2}D\left(y-x,k\right)V\left(y\right)m\left(k,y\right)dy+2\int_{x}^{\infty}\partial_{k}D\left(y-x,k\right)V\left(y\right)\partial_{k}m\left(k,y\right)dy

\displaystyle+\int_{x}^{\infty}D\left(y-x,k\right)V\left(y\right)\partial_{k}^{2}m\left(k,y\right)dy.

(A.48)

From the second equality in (
A.29
) we obtain,

\partial_{k}^{j}D(k,x)=(2i)^{j}\,\int_{0}^{x}\,y^{j}\,e^{2iky}dy,\qquad j=0,1,\dots.

(A.49)

Hence, by (
A.49
)

\left|\partial_{k}^{j}D(k,x)\right|\leq\frac{2^{j}}{j+1}x^{j+1},\qquad j=0,1,\dots.

(A.50)

Moreover, using again (
A.49
), and integrating by parts,

\partial_{k}^{j}D(k,x)=\frac{(2i)^{j}}{2ik}\,\int_{0}^{x}\,dy\,y^{j}\,\partial_{y}e^{2iky}=\frac{(2i)^{j}}{2ik}\left[x^{j}e^{2ikx}-j\int_{0}^{x}\,dy\,y^{j-1}e^{2iky}\right].

(A.51)

By (
A.51
)

\left|\partial_{k}^{j}D(k,x)\right|\leq 2^{j}\,\frac{x^{j}}{|k|},\qquad j=1,2,\dots.

(A.52)

Moreover, by (
A.50
) and (
A.52
)

\left|\partial_{k}^{j}D\left(k,x\right)\right|\leq C\min\left\{\left|x\right|^{j+1},\frac{\left|x\right|^{j}}{\left|k\right|}\right\}\leq C\frac{\left\langle x\right\rangle^{j+1}}{\left\langle k\right\rangle},

(A.53)

for
j=1,2,\dots,
and using (
A.31
), and (
A.39
) we show that the
two first terms on the right-hand side of (
A.48
) are controlled as

\begin{array}[c]{l}\left|\int_{x}^{\infty}\partial_{k}^{2}D\left(y-x,k\right)V\left(y\right)m\left(k,y\right)dy+2\int_{x}^{\infty}\partial_{k}D\left(y-x,k\right)V\left(y\right)\partial_{k}m\left(k,y\right)dy\right|\\
\\
\leq C\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-\delta}\left\|V\right\|_{{L}_{3+\delta}^{1}},\end{array}

(A.54)

for
\delta\geq 0.
The Volterra integral equation (
A.48
) is solved by
successive approximations as follows,

\partial_{k}^{2}m\left(k,x\right)=\partial_{k}^{2}m\left(k,x\right)_{0}+\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\,\partial_{k}^{2}m\left(k,x\right)_{l},

(A.55)

where,

\partial_{k}^{2}m\left(k,x\right)_{0}:=\int_{x}^{\infty}\partial_{k}^{2}D\left(y-x,k\right)V\left(y\right)m\left(k,y\right)dy+2\int_{x}^{\infty}\partial_{k}D\left(y-x,k\right)V\left(y\right)\partial_{k}m\left(k,y\right)\,dy,

(A.56)

and

\partial_{k}^{2}m\left(k,x\right)_{l+1}=\int_{x}^{\infty}D\left(y-x,k\right)V\left(y\right)\partial_{k}^{2}m\left(k,y\right)_{l}dy,\qquad l=1,2,\dots.

(A.57)

Further, by (
A.50
) with
j=0,
(
A.54
), (
A.56
) and
iterating (
A.57
) we obtain,

|\partial_{k}^{2}m\left(k,x\right)_{l}|\leq C\frac{1}{l!}\,\left(\int_{x}^{\infty}\,y|V(y)|\,dy\right)^{l}\,\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-\delta}\left\|V\right\|_{L^{1}_{3+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.58)

Hence, by (
A.54
)-(
A.56
) and (
A.58
) we have that,

|\partial_{k}^{2}m\left(k,x\right)|\leq Ce^{\int_{x}^{\infty}\,y|V(y)|\,dy}\,\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-\delta}\left\|V\right\|_{L^{1}_{3+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.59)

This proves (
A.34
).

Derivating both sides of (
A.44
) two times with respect to
k
we get

\displaystyle\partial_{k}^{2}\partial_{x}m\left(k,x\right)

\displaystyle=-\left(2i\right)^{2}{\displaystyle\int\limits_{x}^{\infty}}\left(y-x\right)^{2}e^{2ik\left(y-x\right)}V\left(y\right)m\left(k,y\right)dy

\displaystyle-4i{\displaystyle\int\limits_{x}^{\infty}}e^{2ik\left(y-x\right)}\left(y-x\right)V\left(y\right)\partial_{k}m_{+}\left(k,y\right)dy-{\displaystyle\int\limits_{x}^{\infty}}e^{2ik\left(y-x\right)}V\left(y\right)\partial_{k}^{2}m_{+}\left(k,y\right)dy.

Then, using (
A.31
), (
A.34
), and (
A.39
), we deduce
(
A.35
). This completes the proof of 3. We prove (
A.36
)
derivating both sides of (
A.48
) and using an iteration argument as in the
proof of (
A.34
). Finally we obtain (
A.37
) arguing in a
similar way as in the proof of (
A.35
).

In the following lemma we collect results on the scattering matrix that we need.

LEMMA A.6

Assume that the potential
V
is self-adjoint, and that the constant boundary matrices
A,B
fulfill (
A.4
), and (
A.5
). Then:

1.

Suppose that
V\in L^{1}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).
Then, the scattering matrix
S(k)
is continuous for
k\in\mathbb{R},
and

\left|S\left(k\right)-S_{0}\left(k\right)\right|\leq C\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1},

(A.60)

where
S_{0}(k)
is the scattering matrix with the boundary condition (
A.3
) given by the boundary matrices
A,B
and the potential identically zero.

2.

Moreover, if
V\in L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
the following relation
is true

\left|S\left(k\right)-S\left(0\right)\right|\leq Ck\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1}.

(A.61)

3.

Further, if
V\in{L}^{1}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),S\in C^{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)

and

\left|\dot{S}\left(k\right)\right|\leq C\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1}.

(A.62)

4.

Furthermore, if
V\in{L}^{1}_{4}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),

S\in C^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
and

\left|\ddot{S}\left(k\right)\right|\leq C\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1}.

(A.63)

Proof.
By the definition of
S(k)
in (
A.17
) and by Theorem 3.8.1
in page 114, Theorem 3.8.13 in page 137, and Theorem 3.8.14 in pages 138 and
139, of
[
6
]

S(k)
is continuous for
k\in\mathbb{R}.
Further
(
A.60
) is proved in equation (3.10.41) in page 198 of
[
6
]
.
This proves 1. Item 2 is proved in Theorem 3.9.15 in pages 189 and 190 of

[
6
]
. Item 3 is proved in Propositions A.1 and A.2 of
[
71
]
.
Let us prove 4. By (
A.16
) and (
A.27
)

\dot{J}(k)=-\dot{m}(-k,0)^{\dagger}B-im(-k,0)^{\dagger}A+ik\dot{m}(-k,0)^{\dagger}A+\dot{m}^{\prime\dagger}A,\qquad k\in\mathbb{R},

(A.64)

where by
\dot{m}(k,x)
we denote the derivative with respect to
k
of

m(k,x).
By Proposition
A.2
,
{J}(k)
is continuous for

k\in\mathbb{R}.
Further, by equation (3.9.17) in page 158 of

[
6
]

|\dot{m}^{\prime}(k,0)|\leq C,\qquad k\in\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}}.

(A.65)

Then, by (
A.39
), (
A.31
),(
A.64
) and (
A.65
)

\dot{J}(k)=O(1)A+O\left(\frac{1}{|k|}\right),\qquad|k|\rightarrow\infty,\text{ }k\in\mathbb{R}.

(A.66)

Derivating (
A.64
) and using (
A.31
), (
A.34
) and
(
A.35
) we show that
\ddot{J}\left(k\right)
is continuous for

k\in\mathbb{R}
and

\ddot{J}\left(k\right)=O(1)A+O\left(\frac{1}{|k|}\right),\qquad|k|\rightarrow\infty,\text{ }k\in\mathbb{R}.

(A.67)

Moreover, derivating (
A.17
) we have

\begin{array}[c]{l}\ddot{S}\left(k\right)=-\ddot{J}\left(-k\right)J\left(k\right)^{-1}-2\dot{J}\left(-k\right)J\left(k\right)^{-1}\dot{J}\left(k\right)J\left(k\right)^{-1}-\\
\\
2J\left(-k\right)J\left(k\right)^{-1}\dot{J}\left(k\right)J\left(k\right)^{-1}\dot{J}\left(k\right)J\left(k\right)^{-1}-J\left(-k\right)J\left(k\right)^{-1}\ddot{J}(k)J\left(k\right)^{-1}.\end{array}

(A.68)

As by Proposition
A.2

J(k)
is continuous and invertible for

k\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
it follows from (
A.68
) that
\ddot{S}(k)

is continuous for
k\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}.
Further, by equation
(3.7.11) in page 113 and equation 3.10.24) in page 195 of
[
6
]
,

J\left(k\right)^{-1}=O(1),\qquad k\rightarrow\infty,\text{ {in}}\,\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}}.

(A.69)

Moreover, by equations (3.7.3) and (3.7.4) in page 113 and equations (3.10.17)
and (3.10.18) in page 194 of
[
6
]
,

J\left(-k\right)J\left(k\right)^{-1}=O(1),\qquad|k|\rightarrow\infty,\text{ }k\in\mathbb{R}.

(A.70)

Furthermore, by equation (3.7.12) in page 113 and taking the inverse in both
sides of equation (3.10.23) in page 195 of
[
6
]
,

AJ\left(k\right)^{-1}=O\left(\frac{1}{|k|}\right),\qquad|k|\rightarrow\infty,\text{ {in}}\,\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}}.

(A.71)

Hence by (
A.66
)-(
A.71
)

\ddot{S}\left(k\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{|k|}\right),\qquad|k|\to\infty,k\in\mathbb{R}.

(A.72)

Moreover, if
J\left(0\right)
is invertible,
\ddot{S}\left(k\right)

is continuous also at
k=0.
In the case where
J\left(0\right)
is not
invertible, it remains for us to control the behaviour of
\ddot{S}\left(k\right)
in a neighborhood of
k=0.
Derivating twice with respect to
k

both sides of (
A.64
) and using (
A.34
)-(
A.37
) we see
that
\dddot{J}(k)
is continuous for
k\in\mathbb{R}
and then,

J\left(k\right)=J\left(0\right)+\dot{J}\left(0\right)k+\frac{\ddot{J}\left(0\right)}{2}k^{2}+\frac{\dddot{J}\left(0\right)}{6}k^{3}+o\left(k^{3}\right),\qquad k\to 0,k\in\mathbb{R},

(A.73)

\dot{J}\left(k\right)=\dot{J}\left(0\right)+\ddot{J}\left(0\right)k+\frac{\dddot{J}\left(0\right)}{2}k^{2}+o\left(k^{2}\right),\qquad k\to 0,k\in\mathbb{R},

(A.74)

and

\ddot{J}(k)=\ddot{J}(0)+\dddot{J}(0)k+o(|k|),\qquad k\to 0,k\in\mathbb{R}.

(A.75)

Moreover, by (
A.31
), (
A.34
) and (
A.36
), for any fixed

a\geq 0,

f(k,a)=f(0,a)+\dot{f}(0,a)k+\ddot{f}(0,a)\frac{k^{2}}{2}+\dddot{f}(0,a)\frac{k^{3}}{6}+o(k^{3}),\qquad k\to 0,k\in\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}}.

(A.76)

In equation (3.9.239) in page 190 of
[
6
]
the following small
k

expansion of
S(k)
is given, assuming that
V\in L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),

S(k)=S(0)+\dot{S}(0)k+o(k),\qquad\qquad k\to 0.

It is easily verified that if
V\in L^{1}_{4}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
the error term

o(k)
can be improved using (
A.76
) to obtain,

S(k)=S(0)+\dot{S}(0)k+\mathcal{H}k^{2}+o\left(k^{2}\right),\qquad\qquad k\to 0,

(A.77)

for some constant matrix
\mathcal{H}.
Furthermore in equation (3.9.237) in
page 189 of
[
6
]
it is proved that if
V\in L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),

J(k)^{-1}=\frac{1}{k}\,\mathcal{M}+\mathcal{E}_{1}+o(1),\qquad k\to 0\,\text{{in}}\,\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}},

(A.78)

where
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{E}_{1}
are constant matrices. It is also
easily checked that if
V\in L^{1}_{4}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
the error term
o(1)

can be improved using (
A.76
) to obtain,

J(k)^{-1}=\frac{1}{k}\,\mathcal{M}+\mathcal{E}_{1}+\mathcal{E}_{2}k+o(k),\qquad k\to 0\,\text{{in}}\,\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}},

(A.79)

for a constant matrix
\mathcal{E}_{2}.
Using (
A.17
) we
write (
A.68
) as follows,

\begin{array}[c]{l}\ddot{S}\left(k\right)=-\ddot{J}\left(-k\right)J\left(k\right)^{-1}-2\dot{J}\left(-k\right)J\left(k\right)^{-1}\dot{J}\left(k\right)J\left(k\right)^{-1}+\\
\\
2S(k)\dot{J}\left(k\right)J\left(k\right)^{-1}\dot{J}\left(k\right)J\left(k\right)^{-1}-S(k)\ddot{J}(k)J\left(k\right)^{-1}.\end{array}.

(A.80)

Then, by (
A.73
)-(
A.75
), (
A.79
) and (
A.80
)

\ddot{S}\left(k\right)=\frac{C_{1}}{k}+\frac{C_{2}}{k^{2}}+g\left(k\right),\qquad k\to 0,

(A.81)

for some constants matrices
C_{1},C_{2}
and a function
g\in C(\mathbb{R}).
Moreover, integrating (
A.81
) we see that for
0<k<\varepsilon,

\dot{S}\left(k\right)=\dot{S}\left(\varepsilon\right)+C_{1}\ln\left(\frac{k}{\varepsilon}\right)+C_{2}\left(\frac{1}{k}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)-\int_{k}^{\varepsilon}g(\lambda)d\lambda.

(A.82)

Since
\dot{S}\left(k\right)
is continuous for all
k\in\mathbb{R}
,
(
A.82
) implies that
C_{1}=C_{2}=0.
Therefore,
S\in C^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
and by (
A.72
), (
A.63
) is true.

We prepare the following results that we need.

LEMMA A.7

Assume that the potential
V
is self-adjoint, and that the constant boundary
matrices
A,B
fulfill (
A.4
), and (
A.5
). Then.

1.

Suppose that
V\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
and that
H
has no negative
eigenvalues. Then,

\left\|k\left(\mathbf{F}^{\pm}\psi\right)\left(k\right)\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left\|\psi\right\|_{{H}^{1}_{A,B}(\mathbb{R}^{+})},\qquad\psi\in{H}^{1}_{A,B}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).

(A.83)

2.

Assume that
V\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
and that
H
has no negative eigenvalues. Then, for all
\psi\in{H}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
that satisfy the boundary condition (
A.3
)

\left\|k^{2}\left(\mathbf{F}^{\pm}\psi\right)\left(k\right)\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left\|\psi\right\|_{{H}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.84)

3.

Suppose that
V\in L^{1}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
and that it admits a regular decomposition (see Definition
1.1
). Then,

\left\|k\partial_{k}\mathbf{F}^{\pm}\psi\left(k\right)\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left\|\psi\right\|_{{H}^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})},\qquad\psi\in{H}^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).

(A.85)

4.

Assume that
V\in L^{1}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).
Then,

\left\|\partial_{k}\mathbf{F}^{\pm}\psi\left(k\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|\psi\|_{L^{2}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})},\qquad\psi\in L^{2}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).

(A.86)

5.

Assume that
V\in L^{1}_{4}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).
Then,

\left\|\partial_{k}^{2}\mathbf{F}^{\pm}\psi\left(k\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|\psi\|_{L^{2}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})},\qquad\psi\in L^{2}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).

(A.87)

Proof.
If
H
has no eigenvalues, its absolutely continuous subspace coincides with

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
and hence, the generalized Fourier maps

\mathbf{F}^{\pm}
are unitary on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
(see
Theorem
A.4
). Further, by (
A.24
) and functional
calculus,

k=\mathbf{F}^{\pm}\sqrt{H}(\mathbf{F}^{\pm})^{\dagger}.

(A.88)

By (
A.88
)

\|k\mathbf{F}^{\pm}\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{2}=\|\sqrt{H}\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}^{2}=h(\psi,\psi),

(A.89)

where
h
is the quadratic form of
H
defined in (
A.10
). Further,
by (
A.10
) and Sobolev’s inequality,

|q(\psi,\psi)|\leq C\|\psi\|_{{H}^{1}_{A,B}(\mathbb{R}^{+})},\qquad\psi\in{H}^{1}_{A,B}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).

(A.90)

Equation (
A.83
) follows from (
A.89
) and (
A.90
). This proves
1. Let us prove 2. By (
A.13
) we have,
\psi\in D[H].
Further, by
(
A.24
)

k^{2}=\mathbf{F}^{\pm}H(\mathbf{F}^{\pm})^{\dagger}.

(A.91)

Then,

\|k^{2}\mathbf{F}^{\pm}\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}=\|H\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|\psi\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.92)

This concludes the proof of 2. Let us prove 3. We give the proof for

\mathbf{F}^{+}.
The case of
\mathbf{F}^{-}
follows similarly. We
calculate

\displaystyle\partial_{k}\Psi\left(-k,y\right)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=e^{-iky}\partial_{k}m\left(k,y\right)^{\dagger}+e^{iky}S\left(-k\right)^{\dagger}\partial_{k}m\left(-k,y\right)^{\dagger}+e^{iky}\partial_{k}S\left(-k\right)^{\dagger}m\left(-k,y\right)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle-iy\left(f\left(k,y\right)^{\dagger}-S\left(-k\right)^{\dagger}f\left(-k,y\right)^{\dagger}\right).

(A.93)

Integrating by parts, we have

\int_{0}^{\infty}ke^{-iky}\partial_{k}m\left(k,y\right)^{\dagger}\psi\left(y\right)dy=-i\partial_{k}m\left(k,0\right)^{\dagger}\psi\left(0\right)-i\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-iky}\partial_{y}\left(\partial_{k}m\left(k,y\right)^{\dagger}\psi\left(y\right)\right)dy.

Then, using (
A.31
) with
\delta>1/2
and (
A.33
), via Sobolev’s
inequality we get

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}ke^{-iky}\partial_{k}m\left(k,y\right)^{\dagger}\psi\left(y\right)dy\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left\|\psi\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.94)

Similarly, by using also (
A.18
) we have

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}ke^{iky}S\left(-k\right)^{\dagger}\partial_{k}m\left(-k,y\right)^{\dagger}\psi\left(y\right)dy\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left\|\psi\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.95)

By (
A.62
) and Parseval’s identify

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}ke^{iky}\dot{S}\left(-k\right)^{\dagger}\psi\left(y\right)dy\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.96)

Further, by (
A.30
) and (
A.62
),

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}ke^{iky}\dot{S}\left(-k\right)^{\dagger}(m(-k,y)^{\dagger}-I)\psi\left(y\right)dy\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.97)

By (
A.96
) and (
A.97
),

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\,ke^{iky}\dot{S}\left(-k\right)^{\dagger}\left(m\left(-k,y\right)^{\dagger}\right)\psi\left(y\right)dy\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left\|\psi\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.98)

Integrating by parts, and recalling that
f(k,y)=e^{iky}\,m(k,y),
we have

\int_{0}^{\infty}kf(k,y)^{\dagger}iy\psi(y)dy=-i\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-iky}\partial_{y}\left(m\left(k,y\right)^{\dagger}iy\psi\left(y\right)\right)dy.

(A.99)

By (
A.32
)

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-iky}\left(\partial_{y}m\left(k,y\right)^{\dagger}\right)y\psi\left(y\right)dy\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.100)

By Parseval’s identity,

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-iky}\psi\left(y\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.101)

By (
A.30
)

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-iky}\left(m\left(k,y\right)^{\dagger}-I\right)\psi\left(y\right)dy\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.102)

It follows from (
A.101
) and (
A.102
),

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-iky}m\left(k,y\right)^{\dagger}\psi\left(y\right)dy\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.103)

In the same way we prove,

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-iky}m\left(k,y\right)^{\dagger}y\partial_{y}\psi(y)\left(y\right)dy\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|y\partial_{y}\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.104)

By (
A.99
), (
A.100
), (
A.103
) and (
A.104
),

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}kf\left(k,y\right)^{\dagger}iy\psi\left(y\right)dy\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left\|\psi\right\|_{H^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.105)

Similarly, using (
A.18
) we show

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}kS\left(-k\right)^{\dagger}iyf\left(-k,x\right)^{\dagger}\psi\left(y\right)dy\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left\|\psi\right\|_{H^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.106)

Hence, (
A.85
) follows from (
A.21
), (
A.93
), (
A.94
),
(
A.95
), (
A.98
), (
A.105
) and (
A.106
).

Let us now prove 4 in the case of
\mathbf{F}^{+}.
The case of
\mathbf{F}^{-}
follows similarly. By (
A.20
)

\partial_{k}\psi(-k,x)^{\dagger}=T_{1}(k,y)+T_{2}(k,y),

(A.107)

where,

T_{1}(k,y):=-iye^{-iky}+iyS(-k)^{\dagger}e^{iky}+e^{iky}\partial_{k}S(-k)^{\dagger},

(A.108)

and

\begin{array}[c]{l}T_{2}(k,y):=e^{-iky}\partial_{k}m(k,y)^{\dagger}+e^{iky}S(-k)^{\dagger}\partial_{k}m(-k,y)\\
\\
+e^{iky}\partial_{k}S(-k)^{\dagger}(m(-k,y)^{\dagger}-I)-iye^{-iky}(m(k,y)^{\dagger}-I)+iye^{iky}S(-k)^{\dagger}(m(-k,y)^{\dagger}-I).\end{array}

(A.109)

By (
A.18
), (
A.62
) and Parseval’s identity,

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\,T_{1}(k,y)\psi(y)\,dy\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|\psi\|_{L^{2}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.110)

Further, by (
A.18
), (
A.62
), (
A.30
) and
(
A.31
),

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\,T_{2}(k,y)\psi(y)\,dy\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.111)

Equation (
A.86
) follows from (
A.21
), (
A.107
),
(
A.110
) and (
A.111
).

Finally, we prove 5 in the case of
\mathbf{F}^{+}.
The case of

\mathbf{F}^{-}
follows in a similar way. By (
A.107
), (
A.108
) and
(
A.109
)

\partial_{k}^{2}\psi(-k,^{2}x)^{\dagger}=T_{3}(k,y)+T_{4}(k,y),

(A.112)

where,

T_{3}(k,y):=-y^{2}e^{-iky}-y^{2}S(-k)^{\dagger}e^{iky}+iye^{iky}\partial_{k}S(-k)^{\dagger}+e^{iky}\partial^{2}_{k}S(-k)^{\dagger}.

(A.113)

and

\begin{array}[c]{l}T_{4}(k,y):=-iye^{-iky}\partial_{k}m(k,y)^{\dagger}+e^{-iky}\partial^{2}_{k}m(k,y)^{\dagger}+iye^{iky}S(-k)^{\dagger}\partial_{k}m(-k,y)+e^{iky}S(-k)^{\dagger}\partial^{2}_{k}m(-k,y)\\
\\
+e^{iky}\partial_{k}S(-k)^{\dagger}\partial_{k}m(-k,y)+iye^{iky}\partial_{k}S(-k)^{\dagger}(m(-k,y)^{\dagger}-I)+e^{iky}\partial^{2}_{k}S(-k)^{\dagger}(m(-k,y)^{\dagger}-I)\\
\\
+e^{iky}(\partial_{k}S(-k)^{\dagger})\partial_{k}m(-k,y)^{\dagger}-y^{2}e^{-iky}(m(k,y)^{\dagger}-I)\\
\\
-iye^{-iky}\partial_{k}m(k,y)^{\dagger}-y^{2}e^{iky}S(-k)^{\dagger}(m(-k,y)^{\dagger}-I)-y^{2}e^{iky}S(-k)^{\dagger}(m(-k,y)^{\dagger}-I)\\
\\
+iye^{iky}\partial_{k}S(-k)^{\dagger}(m(-k,y)^{\dagger}-I)+iye^{iky}S(-k)^{\dagger}\partial_{k}m(-k,y)^{\dagger}.\end{array}

(A.114)

By (
A.18
), (
A.62
), (
A.63
)
and Parseval’s identity,

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\,T_{3}(k,y)\psi(y)\,dy\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|\psi\|_{L^{2}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.115)

Further, by (
A.18
), (
A.62
),
(
A.63
), (
A.30
), (
A.31
), and (
A.34
)

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\,T_{4}(k,y)\psi(y)\,dy\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.116)

Equation (
A.87
) follows from (
A.21
), (
A.112
),
(
A.115
) and (
A.116
).

A.1
The evolution group

In this subsection we obtain a convenient representation of the
restriction of the evolution group to the continuous subspace of the Hamiltonian.

The evolution group is given by,

\mathcal{U}(t):=e^{-itH}.

The restriction of
\mathcal{U}(t)
to the continuous subspace of
H
is given
by,

\mathcal{U}(t)P_{c}(H).

We derive our representation of
\mathcal{U}(t)P_{c}
using the generalized
Fourier map
\mathbf{F}^{+}.
Note, however, that we could also obtain a
representation using
\mathbf{F}^{-}.
To simplify the notation we denote by

\mathbf{F}
the generalized Fourier map
\mathbf{F}^{+}.

By (
A.26
) and as
\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\,\mathbf{F}=P_{c}(H),\mathbf{F}\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}=I
(see Theorem
A.4
) ,

\mathcal{U}(t)P_{\operatorname*{c}}=\mathbf{F}^{\ast}e^{-it\mathcal{M}}\mathbf{F}.

(A.117)

By (
A.21
), (
A.23
), and (
A.117
)

\begin{array}[c]{l}\mathcal{U}(t)\,P_{\operatorname*{c}}(H)\psi(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}f(k,x)e^{-itk^{2}}\mathbf{F}\psi\left(k\right)dk\\
\\
+\int_{0}^{\infty}\,f(-k,x)e^{-itk^{2}}\,S(-k)\,\mathbf{F}\psi\left(k\right)\,dk.\end{array}

(A.118)

Further, for any
\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
it follows from
(
A.118
),

\begin{array}[c]{l}\mathcal{U}(t)\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\phi=\mathcal{U}(t)\,P_{\operatorname*{c}}(H)\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\phi(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}f(k,x)e^{-itk^{2}}\phi\left(k\right)dk\\
\\
+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\,f(-k,x)e^{-itk^{2}}\,S(-k)\,\phi\left(k\right)\,dk\\
\\
=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\,e^{ikx}\,m(k,x)e^{-itk^{2}}\,(\mathcal{E}\phi)(k)\,dk,\end{array}

(A.119)

with, as above,
m\left(k,x\right)=f\left(k,x\right)e^{-ikx}
, and
where we define

\mathcal{E}\phi(k)=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{l}\phi(k),\qquad k\geq 0,\\
S(k)\phi(-k),\qquad k<0.\end{array}\right.

(A.120)

Note that since by (
A.18
)
|S(k)|=1,

\|\mathcal{E}\phi\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})}=\sqrt{2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})},\qquad\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).

(A.121)

Moreover, since by (
A.18
)
S(k)S(-k)=S(k)S(k)^{\dagger}=I,

S(k)(\mathcal{E}\phi)(-k)=(\mathcal{E}\phi)(k),\qquad k\in{\mathbb{R}},\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).

(A.122)

By (
A.119
), we get

\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\,e^{ikx}\,m\left(k,x\right)e^{-itk^{2}}(\mathcal{E}\phi)\left(k\right)dk\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}=\left\|\phi\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})},\qquad\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).

(A.123)

We find it convenient to extend
m(k,x)
to
x\leq 0
as an even function,

m(k,x)=m(k,-x),\qquad x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(A.124)

It follows from a simple calculation that for
t\neq 0

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{ikx}e^{-itk^{2}}m\left(k,x\right)\phi\left(k\right)dk=M\mathcal{D}_{t}\mathcal{W}\left(t\right)\phi,

(A.125)

where we denote

\mathcal{W}\left(t\right)\phi=\sqrt{\frac{it}{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-it\left(k-\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}m\left(k,tx\right)\phi\left(k\right)dk,\qquad t,x\in{\mathbb{R}},

(A.126)

and we recall that,

M:=e^{\frac{ix^{2}}{4t}},\qquad(\mathcal{D}_{t}\phi)(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{it}}\,\phi(xt^{-1}).

(A.127)

We define,

\mathcal{Q}(t):=\mathcal{W}(t)\mathcal{E}.

(A.128)

Hence, by (
A.119
) and (
A.125
) we obtain,

\mathcal{U}\left(t\right)\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\phi=M\mathcal{D}_{t}\mathcal{Q}\left(t\right)\phi,\qquad\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).

(A.129)

Note that (
A.129
) implies,

\mathcal{U}\left(t\right)P_{c}(H)\psi=M\mathcal{D}_{t}\mathcal{Q}\left(t\right)\mathbf{F}\psi,\qquad\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),

(A.130)

where we used that
P_{c}(H)=\mathbf{F}^{\dagger}\mathbf{F}.

Moreover, if
H
has no negative eigenvalues,
P_{c}(H)=I,
and
\mathbf{F}

is unitary in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).
Then, using (
A.129
) and as
M
and

\mathcal{D}_{t}
are unitary in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
we obtain,

\left\|\mathcal{Q}\left(t\right)\phi\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}=\left\|\phi\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.131)

Moreover, using (
A.130
) we see that if
H
has no negative eigenvalues,

\mathcal{Q}^{-1}\left(t\right)=\mathbf{F}\,e^{itH}\,M\mathcal{D}_{t}=e^{itk^{2}}\mathbf{F}M\mathcal{D}_{t},

(A.132)

where we used
\mathbf{F}\mathcal{U}^{-1}(t)=\mathbf{F}e^{itH}=e^{itk^{2}}\,\mathbf{F}.
Further, from the first equality in (
A.132
) we obtain,

\mathbf{F}e^{itH}=\mathcal{Q}^{-1}\,\mathcal{D}_{t}^{-1}\overline{M}.

(A.133)

Moreover, by (
A.132
)

\left\|\mathcal{Q}^{-1}\left(t\right)\phi\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}=\left\|\phi\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})},

(A.134)

and using (
A.18
)

\mathcal{Q}^{-1}\left(t\right)\phi(k)=S\left(k\right)\mathcal{W}_{+}\left(t\right)\phi+\mathcal{W}_{-}\left(t\right)\phi,\qquad k\geq 0,

(A.135)

where

\mathcal{W}_{\pm}\left(t\right)\phi(k)=\sqrt{\frac{t}{2\pi i}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{it\left(k\pm\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}m^{\dagger}\left(\mp k,tx\right)\phi\left(x\right)dx,\qquad k\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(A.136)

Note that in (
A.136
), for convenience, we have defined the quantities

\mathcal{W}_{\pm}(t)
for
k\in{\mathbb{R}},
but that in (
A.135
) we
only use then for
k\geq 0.
We also find it useful to introduce the following
quantity,

\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(t)\phi(k):=S\left(k\right)\mathcal{W}_{+}\left(t\right)\phi+\mathcal{W}_{-}\left(t\right)\phi,\qquad k\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(A.137)

Observe that,

\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(t)\phi(k)=\mathcal{Q}^{-1}\left(t\right)\phi(k),\qquad k\geq 0.

(A.138)

Furthermore, by (
A.18
)

S(k)\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(t)\phi(-k)=\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(t)\phi(k),\qquad k\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(A.139)

In the following proposition we prove some useful results.

PROPOSITION A.8

Assume that
V\in L^{1}_{2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
for some
\delta\geq 0
.
Then.

1.

\|\mathcal{W}\phi\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})}\leq C\|\phi\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})}.

(A.140)

2.

\|\mathcal{W}_{\pm}\phi\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})}\leq C\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.141)

3.

\|\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\phi\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})}\leq C\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.142)

4.

Further, if
V\in L^{1}_{2+\delta},
for some
\delta>1/2,
and
V
admits a regular decomposition (see Definition
1.1
),

\|\partial_{x}\mathcal{W}\phi\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})}\leq C\sqrt{\langle|t|\rangle}\|\phi\|_{H^{1}({\mathbb{R}})}.

(A.143)

Proof.
By (
A.126
)

\begin{array}[c]{l}\mathcal{W}\left(t\right)\phi=e^{-itx^{2}/4}\sqrt{\frac{it}{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{iktx}e^{-itk^{2}}\phi\left(k\right)dk\\
\\
+e^{-itx^{2}/4}\sqrt{\frac{i|t|}{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{iktx}e^{-itk^{2}}[m\left(k,tx\right)-I]\phi\left(k\right)dk.\end{array}

(A.144)

Hence, (
A.140
) follows from (
A.30
), (
A.144
) and Parseval’s
identity. Further, by (
A.136
),

\begin{array}[c]{l}\mathcal{W}_{\pm}\left(t\right)\phi=e^{itk^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{it}{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{\pm iktx}e^{-itx^{2}/4}\phi\left(x\right)dx\\
\\
+e^{itk^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{it}{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{\pm iktx}e^{-itx^{2}/4}[m^{\dagger}\left(\mp k,tx\right)-I]\phi\left(x\right)dx.\end{array}

(A.145)

Then, (
A.141
) follows from (
A.30
), (
A.145
) and Parseval’s
identity. Equation (
A.142
) follows from (
A.18
) and
(
A.141
). Let us prove (
A.143
). Derivating both sides of (
A.126
),
using that

\partial_{x}e^{-it\left(k-\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{k}e^{-it\left(k-\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}},

(A.146)

and integrating by parts, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\mathcal{W}\left(t\right)\phi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{W}\left(t\right)\left(\partial_{k}\phi\right)+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{it}{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-it\left(k-\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}\left(\partial_{k}m\right)\left(k,tx\right)\phi\left(k\right)dk

(A.147)

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\frac{it}{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-it\left(k-\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}t\partial_{x}m\left(k,tx\right)\phi\left(k\right)dk.

Then, using (
A.31
) with
\delta>1/2,
(
A.32
) and (
A.140
) we show

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{x}\mathcal{W}\left(t\right)\phi\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\phi\right\|_{H^{1}({\mathbb{R}})}+C\sqrt{|t|}\left\|\left\langle tx\right\rangle^{-\delta}\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|\left\langle k\right\rangle^{-1}\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|\phi\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})}

\displaystyle+C|t|\sqrt{|t|}\left\|\left\langle tx\right\rangle^{-2-\tilde{\delta}}\right\|_{L^{1}}\left\|\phi\right\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})}.

Hence, (
A.143
) follows from Sobolev’s inequality.

In the following lemma we obtain further properties of
\mathcal{W}_{\pm}
and
of
\mathcal{W}.

LEMMA A.9

Assume that
V\in L^{1}_{2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
for some
\delta>0
.

1.

Suppose that
\phi\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
and that besides being in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),\partial_{k}\phi
admits the following decomposition,
\partial_{k}\phi=\phi_{1}+\phi_{2},
where
\phi_{1}\in L^{q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
and
\phi_{2}\in L^{q_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
for some
q_{1},q_{2},
with
1\leq q_{1},q_{2}<\infty.
Then, we have

\left\|\mathcal{W}_{\pm}\left(t\right)\phi\right\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})}\leq C\left(\left[1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{|t|}}\right]\left\|\phi\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+|t|^{-\frac{1}{2p_{1}}}\left\|\phi_{1}\right\|_{L^{q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+|t|^{-\frac{1}{2p_{2}}}\left\|\phi_{2}\right\|_{L^{q_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right),

(A.150)

and

\left\|\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\left(t\right)\phi\right\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})}\leq C\left(\left[1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{|t|}}\right]\left\|\phi\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+|t|^{-\frac{1}{2p_{1}}}\left\|\phi_{1}\right\|_{L^{q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+|t|^{-\frac{1}{2p_{2}}}\left\|\phi_{2}\right\|_{L^{q_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right),

(A.151)

where
\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{1}}=1,
and
\frac{1}{p_{2}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}=1.

2.

Suppose that
\phi\in H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}),
and that besides being in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}),\partial_{k}\phi
admits the following decomposition,
\partial_{k}\phi=\phi_{1}+\phi_{2},
where
\phi_{1}\in L^{q_{1}}({\mathbb{R}}),
and
\phi_{2}\in L^{q_{2}}({\mathbb{R}}),
for some
q_{1},q_{2},
with
1\leq q_{1},q_{2}<\infty.
Then,

\left\|\mathcal{W}\left(t\right)\phi\right\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})}\leq C\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{|t|}}\right)\left\|\phi\right\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})}+C|t|^{-\frac{1}{2p_{1}}}\left\|\phi_{1}\right\|_{L^{q_{1}}({\mathbb{R}})}+C|t|^{-\frac{1}{2p_{2}}}\left\|\phi_{2}\right\|_{L^{q_{2}}({\mathbb{R}})},

(A.152)

where
\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{1}}=1,
and
\frac{1}{p_{2}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}=1.

Proof.
By (
A.136
)

\mathcal{W}_{\pm}\phi=T_{1}+T_{2},

(A.153)

where

T_{1}:=\sqrt{\frac{t}{2\pi i}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{it\left(k\pm\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}\phi\left(x\right)dx,

(A.154)

and

T_{2}:=\sqrt{\frac{t}{2\pi i}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{it\left(k\pm\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}[m^{\dagger}\left(\mp k,tx\right)-I]\phi\left(x\right)dx.

(A.155)

Since

e^{it\left(k\pm\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}=\pm\frac{2\partial_{x}\left(\left(k\pm\frac{x}{2}\right)e^{it\left(k\pm\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}\right)}{1+2it\left(k\pm\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}},

(A.156)

integrating by parts we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{it\left(k\pm\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}\phi\left(x\right)dx

\displaystyle=\mp\frac{2ke^{itk^{2}}}{1+2itk^{2}}\phi\left(0\right)+\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{4it\left(k\pm\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}e^{it\left(k\pm\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}}{\left(1+2it\left(k\pm\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}\right)^{2}}\phi\left(x\right)dx

\displaystyle\mp\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{2\left(\left(k\pm\frac{x}{2}\right)e^{it\left(k\pm\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}\right)}{1+2it\left(k\pm\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}\partial_{x}\phi(x))dx.

Then, by Hölder’s and Sobolev’s inequalities we get

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{it\left(k\pm\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}\phi\left(x\right)dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{1}{\sqrt{|t|}}\left\|\phi\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+C\left\|\frac{1}{1+|t|x^{2}}\right\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}})}\left\|\phi\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}

\displaystyle+C\left\|\frac{x}{1+|t|x^{2}}\right\|_{L^{p_{1}}({\mathbb{R}})}\left\|\phi_{1}\right\|_{L^{q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+C\left\|\frac{x}{1+|t|x^{2}}\right\|_{L^{p_{2}}({\mathbb{R}})}\left\|\phi_{2}\right\|_{L^{q_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{1}{\sqrt{|t|}}\left\|\phi\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\frac{C}{|t|^{\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\frac{1}{p_{1}}\right)}}\left\|\phi_{1}\right\|_{L^{q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\frac{C}{|t|^{\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\frac{1}{p_{2}}\right)}}\left\|\phi_{2}\right\|_{L^{q_{2}}}.

(A.157)

Hence, by (
A.154
) and (
A.157
)

|T_{1}|\leq C\left(\left\|\phi\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+|t|^{-\frac{1}{2p_{1}}}\left\|\phi_{1}\right\|_{L^{q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+|t|^{-\frac{1}{2p_{2}}}\left\|\phi_{2}\right\|_{L^{q_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right).

(A.158)

By (
A.30
)

|T_{2}|\leq C\frac{1}{\sqrt{|t|}}\left\|\phi\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.159)

Then, (
A.150
) follows from (
A.153
), (
A.158
), and
(
A.159
). Further, (
A.151
) follows from (
A.18
),
(
A.137
), and (
A.150
). Let us prove (
A.152
). By (
A.126
) ,

\mathcal{W}\phi=T_{3}+T_{4},

(A.160)

where

T_{3}:=\sqrt{\frac{t}{2\pi i}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-it\left(k-\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}\phi\left(x\right)dx,

(A.161)

and

T_{4}:=\sqrt{\frac{t}{2\pi i}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-it\left(k-\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}[m^{\dagger}\left(k,tx\right)-I]\phi\left(x\right)dx.

(A.162)

By (
A.156
) with
t
replaced by
-t,
and integrating by parts we have,

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-it\left(k-\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}\phi\left(x\right)dx

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{-4it\left(k-\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}e^{-it\left(k-\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}}{\left(1-2it\left(k-\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}\right)^{2}}\phi\left(x\right)dx

\displaystyle+\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{2\left(\left(k-\frac{x}{2}\right)e^{-it\left(k-\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}\right)}{1-2it\left(k-\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}\partial_{x}\phi(x))dx.

Then, by Hölder’s and Sobolev’s inequalities we get

\displaystyle\left|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-it\left(k-\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}}\phi\left(x\right)dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\frac{1}{1+|t|x^{2}}\right\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}})}\left\|\phi\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+

(A.163)

\displaystyle C\left\|\frac{x}{1+|t|x^{2}}\right\|_{L^{p_{1}}({\mathbb{R}})}\left\|\phi_{1}\right\|_{L^{q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+C\left\|\frac{x}{1+|t|x^{2}}\right\|_{L^{p_{2}}({\mathbb{R}})}\left\|\phi_{2}\right\|_{L^{q_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{|t|}}\left\|\phi\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\frac{C}{|t|^{\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\frac{1}{p_{1}}\right)}}\left\|\phi_{1}\right\|_{L^{q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\frac{C}{|t|^{\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\frac{1}{p_{2}}\right)}}\left\|\phi_{2}\right\|_{L^{q_{2}}}.

Hence, by (
A.161
) and (
A.163
)

|T_{3}|\leq C\left(\left\|\phi\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+|t|^{-\frac{1}{2p_{1}}}\left\|\phi_{1}\right\|_{L^{q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+|t|^{-\frac{1}{2p_{2}}}\left\|\phi_{2}\right\|_{L^{q_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right).

(A.164)

By (
A.30
)

|T_{4}|\leq C\frac{1}{\sqrt{|t|}}\left\|\phi\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.165)

Then (
A.152
) follows from (
A.160
), (
A.164
) and (
A.165
).

We prepare the following lemma.

LEMMA A.10

Assume that the potential
V
is self-adjoint, and that the constant boundary
matrices
A,B
fulfill (
A.4
) and (
A.5
). Then.

1.

Suppose that
V\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).
Then, for all
\psi\in{H}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
satisfying the boundary condition (
A.3
)

\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}\mathcal{U}\left(t\right)\psi\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left\|\psi\right\|_{{H}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.166)

2.

Suppose that
H
has no negative eigenvalues, that
V\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap L_{3}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
and that
V
admits a regular decomposition (see Definition
1.1
). Then, for all
\psi\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
that satisfy the boundary condition (
A.3
),

\left\|x\partial_{x}\mathcal{U}\left(t\right)\psi\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C(1+|t|)\left(\left\|\psi\right\|_{{H}^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\left\|\psi\right\|_{{H}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right).

(A.167)

3.

Assume that
V\in L^{1}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
and that
H
has no negative eigenvalues. Then,

\|xU(t)\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C(1+|t|^{2})\left(\|\psi\|_{L^{2}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|\psi\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right).

(A.168)

4.

Suppose that
V\in L^{1}_{4}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
that it admits a regular decomposition, and that
H
has no negative eigenvalues. Then, for all
\psi\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
that satisfy the boundary condition (
A.3
),

\|x^{2}U(t)\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C(1+|t|^{3})\left(\|\psi\|_{L^{2}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|\psi\|_{H^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|\psi\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right).

(A.169)

5.

Assume that
V\in L^{1}_{2+\tilde{\delta}}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),\tilde{\delta}>0,
that it admits a regular decomposition, and that
H
has no negative eigenvalues. Then, for
\psi\in H^{1}_{A,B}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),

\|\partial_{x}U(t)\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|\psi\|_{H^{1}_{A,B}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

(A.170)

Proof.
Since
V\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),
by (
A.13
), the domain of

H
consists of all those functions in
H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
that satisfy
the boundary condition (
A.3
). Hence
\psi\in D[H].
Further,

\partial_{x}^{2}\mathcal{U}\left(t\right)\psi=(-H+V)\mathcal{U}\left(t\right)\psi=-\mathcal{U}\left(t\right)H\psi+V\mathcal{U}\left(t\right)\psi.

Then,

\left\|\partial_{x}^{2}\mathcal{U}\left(t\right)\psi\right\|_{{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq\|H\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|V\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|\psi\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})},

where we used that as
D[H]\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),

\|H\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|\psi\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}.

This completes the proof of (
A.166
). Let us now prove
(
A.167
). As
H
has no negative eigenvalues,
P_{c}(H)=I(seeTheorem~\ref{theospec}.
Then, from (
A.118
) we have

x\partial_{x}\mathcal{\mathcal{U}}(t)\,\psi(x)=T_{1}+T_{2},

(A.171)

where,

T_{1}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{ikx}\partial_{k}\left(ke^{-itk^{2}}\mathbf{F}\psi\left(k\right)\right)dk+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\,e^{-ikx}\partial_{k}\left(ke^{-itk^{2}}\,S(-k)\,\mathbf{F}\psi\left(k\right)\right)dk,

(A.172)

and,

\begin{array}[c]{l}T_{2}=\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\,xe^{-itk^{2}}\left(\,e^{ikx}(m(k,x)-I)-e^{-ikx}(m(-k,x)-I)\,S(-k)\right)k\mathbf{F}\psi(k)\,dk\\
\\
+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\,xe^{-itk^{2}}\left(\,e^{ikx}\partial_{x}m(k,x)+e^{-ikx}\partial_{x}m(-k,x)\,S(-k)\right)\mathbf{F}\psi(k)\,dk.\end{array}

(A.173)

By (
A.18
),(
A.22
), item 3 of Lemma
A.6
, items
2 and 3 of Lemma
A.7
, and Parseval’s identity, we estimate,

\begin{array}[c]{l}\|T_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq\|\partial_{k}\left(ke^{-itk^{2}}\mathbf{F}\psi\left(k\right)\right)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|\partial_{k}\left(ke^{-itk^{2}}\,S(-k)\,\mathbf{F}\psi\left(k\right)\right)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\\
\\
\leq C(1+|t|)\left(\|\psi\|_{H^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|\psi\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\right).\end{array}

(A.174)

Moreover, by (
A.18
), (
A.30
) with
\delta>1/2
, (
A.32
),
and (
A.83
),

\|T_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq\|\psi\|_{H^{1}({\mathbb{R}})}.

(A.175)

Hence, we deduce (
A.167
) from (
A.171
), (
A.174
) and (
A.175
).

Let us now prove item 3. Let
g\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
satisfy

g(k)=1,|k|\leq 1,g(k)=0,|k|\geq 2.

)
with

(A.178)

(A.181)

\\
\left.+e^{-ikx}\,e^{-isk^{2}}x(m(-k,x)-1)\,S(-k)k^{2}\mathbf{F}\psi(k)\right]\,g(k^{2})dk.\end{array}

T_{4}(t)=T_{7}(t)+T_{8}(t),

(A.191)

), (
A.185
) and
(
A.191
). Let us now prove item 4. Using (
